System F ω μ F^{\mu}_{\omega} with Context-free Session Types Thanks: Support for this research was provided by the Fundação para a Ciência e a Tecnologia through project SafeSessions, ref. PTDC/CCI-COM/6453/2020, and by the LASIGE Research Unit, ref. UIDB/00408/2020 and ref. UIDP/00408/2020. Affiliation: LASIGE, Faculdade de Ciências, Universidade de Lisboa, Portugal E-mail {dfdcosta,afmordido,dmpocas,vmvasconcelos}@ciencias.ulisboa.pt
Diana Costa, Andreia Mordido, Diogo Poças, Vasco T. Vasconcelos

Abstract
We study increasingly expressive type systems, from F μ F^{\mu} —an extension of the polymorphic lambda calculus with equirecursive types—to F ω μ ; F^{\mu;}_{\omega} —the higher-order polymorphic lambda calculus with equirecursive types and context-free session types. Type equivalence is given by a standard bisimulation defined over a novel labelled transition system for types. Our system subsumes the contractive fragment of F ω μ F^{\mu}_{\omega} as studied in the literature. Decidability results for type equivalence of the various type languages are obtained from the translation of types into objects of an appropriate computational model: finite-state automata, simple grammars and deterministic pushdown automata. We show that type equivalence is decidable for a significant fragment of the type language. We further propose a message-passing, concurrent functional language equipped with the expressive type language and show that it enjoys preservation and absence of runtime errors for typable processes.



1
Introduction

Extensions of the
\lambda
-calculus to include increasingly sophisticated type
structures have been extensively studied and have led to systems whose
importance is widely recognized: System
F

[
60
]
, System
F^{\mu}

[
30
]
, System

F_{\omega}

[
36
]
, System
F^{\mu}_{\omega}

[
16
]
. Ideally, we would like to combine a

wishlist
of type structures and get a super-powerful system with vast
expressiveness. However, the expressiveness of types is naturally limited by the
universe where they are supposed to live: programming languages. Expressive type
systems pose challenges to compilers that other (less expressive) types do not
even reveal; one such example is type equivalence checking.

System
F
can be enriched with different type constructors for specifying
communication protocols. We analyse the impact of combinations of such
constructors on the type equivalence problem.
In order to do so, we extend System
F
with session
types
[
42
,
43
,
67
]
.
Session types
provide for detailed protocol specifications in the form of types.
Traditional recursive session types are limited to tail recursion, thus failing
to capture all protocols whose traces cannot be characterized by regular
languages. Context-free session types overcome this limitation by extending
types with a notion of sequential composition,
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{T};{U}}}

[
3
,
68
]
. The set of types together with the
binary operation
;
constitutes a monoid, for which a new type,
\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{Skip}}}
,
acts as the neutral element and
\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{End}}}
acts as an absorbing element.

The regular recursive type

{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\mu}}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\alpha}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\colon}}{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{\textsc{s}}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{.\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\&}}\{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{Done}}}\colon\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{End}}},\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{More}}}\colon?\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{Int}}};\alpha\}}}

describes an integer
stream
as seen from the point of view of the
consumer. It offers a choice between
\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{Done}}}
—after which the channel must
be closed, as witnessed by type
\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{End}}}
—and
\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{More}}}
—after which an integer
value must be received, followed by the rest of the stream. Types are
categorised by
kinds
, so that we know that the recursion variable

{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\alpha}}
is of kind session—denoted by
s
—and, thus, can be used
with semicolon.
Instead, we might want to write a type with a more
context-free
flavour.
The type

{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\mu}}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\alpha}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\colon}}{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{\textsc{s}}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{.\,\&\{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{Leaf}}}:\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{Skip}}},\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{Node}}}:\alpha;?\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{Int}}};\alpha\}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{;}}\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{End}}}
describes a protocol for the type-safe
streaming of integer
trees
on channels. The continuation to the
\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{Leaf}}}

option is
\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{Skip}}}
, where no communication occurs but the channel is still open
for further composition. The continuation to the
\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{Node}}}
choice receives a left
subtree, an integer at the root and a right subtree. In either case, once the
whole tree is received, the channel must be closed, as witnessed by the final

\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{End}}}
.
Beyond first-order context-free session types (where only basic types are
exchanged)
[
3
,
68
]
we may be interested in
higher-order session types capable of exchanging values of complex
types
[
21
]
.
A goal of this paper is the integration of higher-order context-free session
types into system
F^{\mu}_{\omega}
. We want to be able to abstract the type that
is received on a tree channel, which is now possible by writing
\char 12\relax\alpha{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{\textsc{t}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\mu}}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\beta}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\colon}}{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{\textsc{s}}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{.\,\&\{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{Leaf}}}:\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{Skip}}},\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{Node}}}:\beta;?\alpha;\beta\}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{;}}\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{End}}}
,
where
t
is the kind of functional types.

A form of abstraction over session types was formerly proposed by Das
et al.
[
25
,
26
]
via (nested)
parametric polymorphism.
In the notation of Das et al., we can write a type equation

{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{Stream}}}}\langle{\alpha}\rangle}}}}}\;{\doteq}\;{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\&}}\{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{Done}}}\colon\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{End}}},\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{More}}}\colon?\alpha;{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{Stream}}}}\langle{\alpha}\rangle}}\}}}}

that abstracts the type being received on a stream channel.
To write the same type using abstraction, we can think of
\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{Stream}}}
as a function of its parameter
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\alpha}}
,

{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{Stream}}}}}}\;{\doteq}\;{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\char 12\relax\alpha{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{\textsc{t}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\&}}\{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{Done}}}\colon\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{End}}},\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{More}}}\colon?\alpha;{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{Stream}}}}\,{\alpha}}}\}}}}}
;
we can then rewrite
\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{Stream}}}
using the
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\mu}}
-operator,

\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{Stream}}}=\char 12\relax\alpha{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{\textsc{t}}}{({\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\mu}}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\beta}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\colon}}{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{\textsc{s}}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{.\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\&}}\{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{Done}}}\colon\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{End}}},\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{More}}}\colon{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{?{\alpha}}}.{\beta}}}\}}})}.

Das et al. proved that parametrized type definitions
over regular session types are strictly more expressive than context-free
session types. To some extent, this analogy guides our approach: if adding
abstraction (via parametric polymorphism) to regular types leads to nested types, what exactly does it
mean to add abstraction (via a type-level
\lambda
-operator) to context-free types? Throughout this paper we analyse
several increments to System
F^{\mu}
that culminate in adding

\lambda
-abstraction to context-free session types.

One of our focuses is necessarily the analysis of the type equivalence problem.
The uncertainty about the decidability of this problem over recursive parametric
types goes back to the
1970s
[
18
,
63
]
. Although the
type equivalence problem for parametric (nested) session types and context-free
session types is decidable, that for the combination of abstractions over
context-free types may no longer be. In fact, this analysis constitutes an
interesting journey towards a better understanding of the role of higher-order
polymorphic recursion in presence of sequential composition, as well as the
gains (and losses) resulting from combining abstraction with arbitrary (rather
than tail) recursion.

Ultimately, decidability is not a sufficiently valuable measure regarding a type
system’s
practicality
. We look for type systems that may be incorporated
into compilers. For that reason, we are interested in algorithms for type
equivalence checking. Equivalence in
F^{\mu}_{\omega}
alone is already at least as
expressive as deterministic pushdown automata. If we restrict recursion to the
monomorphic case (requiring recursion variables to denote proper types, that is
of kind
s
or
t
, collectively denoted by
{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{*}}
)
we lower the complexity of type equivalence to that of equivalence for
finite-state automata. The extension with context-free session types is slightly
more complex. In order to obtain “good” algorithms, we restrict the recursion
to the monomorphic case,
arriving at classes
F^{\mu_{\ast}}_{\omega},F^{\mu_{\ast};}_{\omega}
. Now the type equality
problem for
F^{\mu_{\ast};}_{\omega}
translates to the equivalence problem for simple
grammars, which is still decidable
[
6
,
33
]
.
Since
F^{\mu_{\ast};}_{\omega}
subsumes
F^{\mu_{\ast}}_{\omega}
, our proof of the
decidability of type equivalence serves as an alternative to that of Cai
et al.
[
16
]
(restricted to contractive types).

Higher-order polymorphism allows for the definition of type operators and the
internalisation of various (session-type) constructs that would otherwise be
offered as built-in constructors. In this way, we are able to internalise basic
session-type constructors such as sequential composition
;
and the

\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{Dual}}}
type operator (which reverses the direction of communication between
parties).
Duality is often treated as an external macro. Gay
et al.
[
34
]
explore different ways of handling
the dual operator, all in a monomorphic setting. In the presence of polymorphism
the dual operator cannot be fully eliminated without introducing co-variables.
Internalisation offers a much cleaner solution.

Due to the presence of sequential composition, regular trees are
not
a
powerful enough model for representing types (
type

TreeC

a
in

Section

2
is an example).
The main technical challenge
when combining System
F^{\mu}_{\omega}
and context-free session types is making sure
that the resulting model can still be represented by simple grammars, so that
type equivalence may be decided by a practical algorithm.
The difficulties arise with renaming bound variables.
For infinite types, both renaming with fresh variables and using de Bruijn
indices may create an infinite number of distinct variables, which makes the
construction of a simple grammar simply impossible. For example, take the type

\char 12\relax\alpha{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{\textsc{t}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\mu}}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\gamma}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\colon}}{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{\textsc{t}}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{.\,\char 12\relax\beta{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{\textsc{t}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\alpha\rightarrow\gamma}}}}}}
,
which stands for the infinite type

\char 12\relax\alpha{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{\textsc{t}}}{\char 12\relax\beta{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{\textsc{t}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\alpha\rightarrow\char 12\relax\beta{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{\textsc{t}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\alpha\rightarrow\char 12\relax\beta{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{\textsc{t}}}..}}}}}}}

Renaming this type using a fresh variable at each step would result in a type of
the form

\char 12\relax{\upsilon_{1}}{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{\textsc{t}}}{\char 12\relax{\upsilon_{2}}{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{\textsc{t}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\upsilon_{1}\rightarrow\char 12\relax{\upsilon_{3}}{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{\textsc{t}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\upsilon_{1}\rightarrow\char 12\relax{\upsilon_{4}}{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{\textsc{t}}}..}}}}}}}
,
requiring infinitely many variables. Similarly, de Bruijn
indices
[
28
]
yield a type of the form

{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\lambda_{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{\textsc{t}}}\lambda_{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{\textsc{t}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{1\rightarrow\lambda_{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{\textsc{t}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{2\rightarrow\lambda_{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{\textsc{t}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{3\rightarrow\ldots}}}}}}}}
that requires an infinite number of
natural indices.
We thus introduce
minimal renaming
that uses the least amount of variable
names as possible (cf. Gauthier and Pottier
[
30
]
).
This ensures that only finitely many terminal symbols are necessary, allowing
for translating types into simple grammars.

Type languages live in term languages and we propose a term language to consume

F^{\mu;}_{\omega}
types. Based on Almeida
et al.
[
2
]
, we introduce a message-passing
concurrent programming language. Type checking is decidable if type equivalence
is, and it is, in particular, for
F^{\mu_{\ast};}_{\omega}
.

The main contributions of this paper are as follows.

•

The integration of (higher-order) context-free session types into system

F^{\mu}_{\omega}
, dubbed
F^{\mu;}_{\omega}
.

•

A semantic definition of type equivalence via a labelled transition system.

•

The identification of a suitable fragment of System
F^{\mu;}_{\omega}
for which type equivalence is reduced to the bisimilarity of simple grammars.

•

A proof that type equivalence on the full System
F^{\mu;}_{\omega}
is at least as hard as bisimilarity of deterministic pushdown automata.

•

The first internalisation of the
\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{Dual}}}
type operator in a type language.

•

A term language to consume
F^{\mu;}_{\omega}
types and an accompanying metatheory.

The type system presented in the paper combines three constructions: sequential composition of session types, higher-order kinds via type-level abstraction and application, and higher-order recursion.
Prior to our work there is the system by Almeida et al.
[
6
]
which incorporates sequential composition and (first-order) recursion, but no higher-order kinds.
There is also the system by Cai et al.
[
16
]
which incorporates higher-order kinds and higher-order recursion, but no sequential composition.
Our system is the first to incorporates all three constructions.
Although some of the results are incremental and generalize results from the literature, the main technical challenge is understanding the border past which they don’t hold anymore.
For example, “just” including higher-order kinds into the system by Almeida et al. does not work, since we need to pay close attention to variable names, making sure that type equivalence is invariant with respect to alpha-conversion (renaming of bound variables).
This required us to define a novel notion of renaming, inspired by Gauthier and Pottier
[
30
]
.
Similarly, “just” including sequential composition into the system of Cai et al. does not work, since finite-state automata (or regular trees) are not enough to capture the expressive power of the new type system,
even
when restricted to first-order recursion.
This required us to look at the more expressive framework of simple grammars, and introduce a translation from types to words of a simple grammar.

The rest of the paper is organised as follows. The next section motivates the
type language and introduces the term language with an example.
Section

3

introduces System
F^{\mu;}_{\omega}
,
Section

4
discusses type
equivalence and
Section

5
shows that type equivalence is
decidable for a fragment of the type language.
Section

6

presents the term language and its metatheory.
Section

7
discusses
related work and
Section

8
concludes the paper with pointers for
future work.

2
Motivation

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\llparenthesis{}\rrparenthesis}}\;::=\;{}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\{\}}}\;\mid\;{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\langle{}\rangle}}

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\sharp}}\;::=\;{}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{?}}\;\mid\;{}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{!}}

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\odot}}\;::=\;{}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\&}}\;\mid\;{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\hskip-0.43057pt\oplus\hskip-0.43057pt}}

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}\;::=\;

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T\rightarrow T}}\;\mid\;{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\llparenthesis\overline{{l_{i}}\colon{T_{i}}}\rrparenthesis}}\;\mid\;{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\forall{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\alpha}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\colon}}{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{\kappa}}}}.\,T}}\;\mid\;{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\mu}}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\alpha}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\colon}}{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{\kappa}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{.\,T}}\;\mid\;{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\alpha}}

\displaystyle(F^{\mu})

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}\;::=\;

\displaystyle(F^{\mu})\;\mid\;{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\sharp{T}}}.{T}}}\;\mid\;{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\odot}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\{\overline{{l_{i}}\colon{T_{i}}}\}}}\;\mid\;\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{End}}}

\displaystyle(F^{\mu\cdot})

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}\;::=\;

\displaystyle(F^{\mu})\;\mid\;{}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\sharp{T}}}\;\mid\;{}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\odot}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\{\overline{{l_{i}}\colon{T_{i}}}\}}}\;\mid\;\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{End}}}\;\mid\;{}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{T};{T}}}\;\mid\;\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{Skip}}}

\displaystyle(F^{\mu;})

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}\;::=\;

\displaystyle(F^{M})\;\mid\;\char 12\relax\alpha{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{\kappa}}T\;\mid\;{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{T}\,{T}}}

\displaystyle(F^{M}_{\omega}),\quad M\;::=\;\mu\ ,\mu\cdot\ ,\mu;

Figure 1
:
Six
F
-systems.

Our goal is to study type systems that combine equirecursion, higher-order
polymorphism, and higher-order context-free session types and their
incorporation in programming languages.

Extensions of
F
.

Figure

1
motivates the construction by proposing six different type
syntaxes, culminating with
F^{\mu;}_{\omega}
.
The initial system,
F^{\mu}
, includes well-known basic type
operators
[
57
]
: functions
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T\rightarrow U}}
, records

{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\{\overline{{l_{i}}\colon{T_{i}}}\}}}
and variants
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\langle\overline{{l_{i}}\colon{T_{i}}}\rangle}}
. Type
\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{Unit}}}
is short
for
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\{\}}}
, the empty record; we can imagine that
\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{Unit}}}
stands in place of an arbitrary scalar type such as
\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{Int}}}
and

\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{Bool}}}
. We also include variable names
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\alpha}}
, type quantification

{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\forall{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\alpha}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\colon}}{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{\kappa}}}}.\,T}}
and recursion
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\mu}}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\alpha}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\colon}}{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{\kappa}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{.\,T}}
.
In order to control type formation, all variable bindings must be kinded with
some kind
{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{\kappa}}
, even if for the initial system,
F^{\mu}
, we only use the
functional kind
t
.

We then build on
F^{\mu}
by considering (regular, tail recursive) session types;
we represent the resulting system by
F^{\mu\cdot}
.
For example
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{?{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{Int}}}}}}.{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{!{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{Bool}}}}}}.{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{End}}}}}}}}}
is a type for a channel endpoint that
receives an integer, sends a boolean, and terminates.
At this point we introduce a kind
s
of session types to
restrict the ways in which we can combine session and functional types together.
For example, a well-formed type
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{?{T}}}.{U}}}
is of kind
s
and requires
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U}}

to be also of kind
s
(whereas
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}
can be of kind
{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{*}}
, that is
s

or
t
).
An example of an infinite session type is

{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\mu}}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\alpha}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\colon}}{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{\textsc{s}}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{.\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{!{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{Int}}}}}}.{\alpha}}}}}
that endlessly outputs integer
values. For a more elaborate example consider the type

\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{IntStream}}}={\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\mu}}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\alpha}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\colon}}{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{\textsc{s}}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{.\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\&}}\{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{Done}}}\colon\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{End}}},\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{More}}}\colon{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{?{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{Int}}}}}}.{\alpha}}}\}}}

that specifies a channel endpoint for receiving a (finite or infinite) stream of
integer values.
Communication ends after choice
\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{Done}}}
is selected.

The next step of our construction takes us to context-free session types; the
resulting system is denoted by
F^{\mu;}
. We introduce a new construct for
sequential composition
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{T};{U}}}
, and a new type
\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{Skip}}}
, acting as the
neutral element of sequential composition
[
68
]
. The
message constructors are now unary (
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{?{T}}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{!{T}}}
) rather than binary.
In System
F^{\mu;}
we distinguish between the traditional
\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{End}}}
type and the

\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{Skip}}}
type. These types have different behaviours:
\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{End}}}
terminates a
channel, while
\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{Skip}}}
allows for further communication. Type equality is more
subtle for context-free session types, because of the monoidal semantics of
sequential composition. It is derivable from the following axioms:

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{Skip}}}};{T}}}}}

\displaystyle\sim{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}

\displaystyle\text{Neutral element}

(1)

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{End}}}};{T}}}}}

\displaystyle\sim{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{End}}}}}

\displaystyle\text{Absorbing element}

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{({\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{T};{U}}})};{V}}}}}

\displaystyle\sim{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{T};{({\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{U};{V}}})}}}}

\displaystyle\text{Associativity}

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\odot}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\{\overline{{l_{i}}\colon{T_{i}}}\}}}};{U}}}}}

\displaystyle\sim{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\odot}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\{\overline{{l_{i}}\colon{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{T_{i}};{U}}}}}\}}}}

\displaystyle\text{Distributivity}

Although the syntax of
F^{\mu\cdot}
is not formally included in the syntax of

F^{\mu;}
, we can embed recursive session types into context-free session types
by mapping
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\sharp{T}}}.{U}}}
into
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\sharp{T}}}};{U}}}
. It is well-known that
context-free session types allow for higher computational expressivity: while

F^{\mu}
and
F^{\mu\cdot}
can be represented via finite-state automata,
F^{\mu;}

can only be represented with simple grammars

[
6
,
33
]
.

To finalise our construction, we include type abstraction
\char 12\relax\alpha{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{\kappa}}T

and type application
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}\ {\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U}}
. Again, type abstraction binds a variable which
must be kinded. Kinds can now be of higher-order
{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{\kappa}}}\Rightarrow{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{\kappa}}^{\prime}}}}
. For
each of the three systems
F^{\mu}
,
F^{\mu\cdot}
,
F^{\mu;}
we arrive at a
higher-order version, respectively
F^{\mu}_{\omega}
,
F^{\mu\cdot}_{\omega}
,
F^{\mu;}_{\omega}

(all of which we represent as
F^{M}_{\omega}
). In System
F^{\mu\cdot}_{\omega}
, for
example, we can specify channels for receiving (finite or infinite) sequences of
values of arbitrary (but fixed) types,

\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{Stream}}}=\char 12\relax\alpha{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{\textsc{t}}}{({\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\mu}}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\beta}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\colon}}{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{\textsc{s}}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{.\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\&}}\{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{Done}}}\colon\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{End}}},\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{More}}}\colon{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{?{\alpha}}}.{\beta}}}\}}})}

where
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\alpha}}
can be instantiated with the desired type; in particular,

{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{Stream}}}}\,{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{Int}}}}}}
would be equivalent to the aforementioned
\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{IntStream}}}
.

It turns out that the expressive power of general higher-order systems

F^{M}_{\omega}
is too large for practical purposes. Even the simplest case

F^{\mu}_{\omega}
is at least as expressive as deterministic pushdown automata (or
equivalently, first-order grammars), for which known equivalence algorithms are
notoriously impractical. By impractical we mean that, although there exists a
proof of decidability (due to Sénizergues
[
61
]
,
later improved by Stirling and
Jancar
[
46
,
65
]
), the
underlying algorithm is rather complex. To the best of our knowledge, there
is no practical implementation of an algorithm to decide the equivalence of
deterministic pushdown automata. This is essentially due to polymorphic
recursion, which can be encoded by a higher-order
\mu
-operator (we provide an example at the end of
Section

5
). Therefore, it makes sense to restrict the kind
{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{\kappa}}
of the
recursion operator
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\mu}}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\alpha}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\colon}}{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{\kappa}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{.\,T}}
. We use the notation
\mu_{\ast}
to
mean the subclass of types written using only
{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{*}}
-kinded recursion, i.e.,
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\mu}}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\alpha}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\colon}}{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{\textsc{t}}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{.\,T}}
or
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\mu}}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\alpha}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\colon}}{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{\textsc{s}}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{.\,T}}
.

F^{\mu}
F^{\mu\cdot}
F^{\mu;}
F^{\mu_{\ast}}_{\omega}
F^{\mu_{\ast}\cdot}_{\omega}
F^{\mu_{\ast};}_{\omega}
F^{\mu}_{\omega}
F^{\mu\cdot}_{\omega}
F^{\mu;}_{\omega}
finite-state
automata
simple
grammars
\geq

deterministic
pushdown

automata

Figure 2
:
Relation between the main classes of types in this paper (arrows denote strict inclusions).

Figure

2
summarizes the main relations between the classes of types
in our paper. Firstly, we obtain a lattice where the expressive power increases
as we travel down (from functional to session to context-free session types) and
right (from simple polymorphism to higher-order polymorphism with monomorphic recursion
to arbitrary recursion). Four of the classes can be represented
using finite-state automata (up to
F^{\mu_{\ast}\cdot}_{\omega}
). By including sequential
composition (
F^{\mu;}
and
F^{\mu_{\ast};}_{\omega}
) we are still able to represent types using
simple grammars. Once we allow for arbitrary recursion, the expressiveness of
our model requires the computational power of deterministic pushdown automata.

Programming with
F^{\mu;}_{\omega}
.

We now turn our attention to the term language, a message passing, concurrent
functional language, equipped with context-free session types.
Start with a stream of values of type
a
. Such a stream, when seen
from the side of the reader, offers two choices:
Done
and

More
. In the former case the interaction is over; in the latter the
reader reads a value of type
a
, as in
?
a
, and recurses.
This is the stream type we have seen before only that, rather than closing the
channel endpoint (with type
End
), it terminates with type

Skip
, so that it may be sequentially composed with other types. In
this informal introduction to the term language we omit the kinds of type
variables.

⬇

type

Stream

a

=

&{
Done
:

Skip
,

More
:

?
a

;

Stream

a
}

A fold channel, as seen from the side of the folder, is a type of the following
form. We assume that application binds tighter than semicolon, that is, type

;

b

;

is interpreted as
(

a
)

;

;

the result of the fold. The type terminates with

Function

consumes the initial part of the channel and
passes the rest of the channel to the recursive function
foldS
that
consumes the whole stream while accumulating the fold value. In the end, when
branch
Done
is selected, the fold value is written on the channel
and the channel closed.
In general, the channel operators—
receive
,
send
,

select
—return the same channel in the form of a new identifier. It
is customary to reuse the identifier name—
c
in the example, as in

(
f
,

c
)

=

receive

c
—since it denotes the same channel. Syntax

c
\triangleright
...
hides the continuation channel. The case for the external
choice—
match
—also returns the continuation (in each branch) so
that interaction on the channel endpoint may proceed.

We may now write different clients for the
foldServer
. Examples
include a client that generates a stream from a pair of integer values (denoting
an interval); another that generates the stream from a list of values; and yet
another that generates the stream from a binary tree.
We propose a further client. Consider the type of a channel that exchanges trees
in a serialized format

Figure 4
:
Type constants and kinds.

This section introduces in detail System
F^{\mu;}_{\omega}
, an extension of
System
F^{\mu}_{\omega}
incorporating higher-order context-free session types.
The syntax of types is presented in
Fig.

4
.
A type is either a constant
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\iota}}

(as in
Fig.

4
), a type variable
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\alpha}}
, an abstraction

{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\lambda{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\alpha}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\colon}}{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{\kappa}}}}.{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}}}}
or an application
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{T}\,{U}}}
.
Besides incorporating the standard session type constructors as constants, system

F^{\mu;}_{\omega}
also includes
\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{Dual}}}
as a constant for a type operator mapping a
session type to its dual. Note also that
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\forall{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\alpha}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\colon}}{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{\kappa}}}}.\,T}}
is
syntactic sugar for
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\forall_{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{\kappa}}({\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\lambda{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\alpha}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\colon}}{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{\kappa}}}}.{T}}})}}
.
Analogously,
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\mu}}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\alpha}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\colon}}{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{\kappa}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{.\,T}}
abbreviates

{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\mu}}_{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{\kappa}}}}({\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\lambda{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\alpha}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\colon}}{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{\kappa}}}}.{T}}})}}
. This simplifies our analysis
as lambda abstraction becomes the only binding operator.

A distinction between session and functional types is made resorting to
kinds
s
and
t
, respectively. These are the kinds of proper types,

{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{*}}
; we use the symbol
{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{\kappa}}
to represent either the kind of a proper
type or that of a type operator, of the form
{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{\kappa}}}\Rightarrow{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{\kappa}}^{\prime}}}}
. A
kinding context
\Delta
stores kinds for type variables using bindings of the
form
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\alpha}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\colon}}{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{\kappa}}}}
. Notation
\Delta+{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\alpha}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\colon}}{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{\kappa}}}}
denotes the
update of kinding context
\Delta
, defined as

(\Delta,{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\alpha}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\colon}}{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{\kappa}}}})+{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\alpha}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\colon}}{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{\kappa}}^{\prime}}}=\Delta,{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\alpha}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\colon}}{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{\kappa}}^{\prime}}}
and
\Delta+{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\alpha}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\colon}}{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{\kappa}}}}=\Delta,{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\alpha}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\colon}}{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{\kappa}}}}

when
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\alpha}}\not\in\Delta
.

Type renaming
\operatorname{rename}_{S}({\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}})

\displaystyle\operatorname{rename}_{S}({\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\iota}})

\displaystyle={\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\iota}}

\displaystyle\operatorname{rename}_{S}({\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\alpha}})

\displaystyle={\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\alpha}}

\displaystyle\operatorname{rename}_{S}(\char 12\relax{\alpha}{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{\kappa}}}{T})

\displaystyle=\char 12\relax{\upsilon}{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{\kappa}}}{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\operatorname{rename}_{S}(}}}T{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{[}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\upsilon}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{/}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\alpha}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{]}}})\quad\text{where }{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\upsilon}}=\operatorname{first}_{S}(\char 12\relax{\alpha}{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{\kappa}}}{T})

\displaystyle\operatorname{rename}_{S}({\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{T}\,{U}}})

\displaystyle=\operatorname{rename}_{S\cup\operatorname{fv}({\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U}})}({\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}})\operatorname{rename}_{S}({\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U}})

Figure 5
:
Type renaming.

To define type formation, we require a few notions. Firstly comes the
notion of
renaming
, adapted from Gauthier and Pottier
[
30
]
and presented in
Fig.

5
.
Renaming essentially replaces a type
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}
by a minimal alpha-conversion of
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}
. By alpha-conversion we mean that
\operatorname{rename}_{S}({\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}})
renames bound variables in
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}
. By “minimal” we mean that each bound variable is renamed to its lowest possible value.
We assume at our disposal a countable well-ordered set of type variables

\{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\upsilon_{1}}},\ldots,{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\upsilon_{n}}},\ldots\}
.
In
\operatorname{rename}_{S}({\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}})
, parameter
S
is a set containing type variables
unavailable for renaming; in the outset of the renaming process
S
is the empty
set, since all variables are available. In that case the subscript
S
is often
omitted. The case for lambda abstraction renames the bound variable by the smallest variable not in the set

S\cup\operatorname{fv}(\char 12\relax{\alpha}{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{\kappa}}}{T})
, which we denote by
\operatorname{first}_{S}(\char 12\relax{\alpha}{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{\kappa}}}{T})
.

Renaming is what allows us to check whether type abstractions
\char 12\relax\alpha{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{\kappa}}T
,
\char 12\relax\beta{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{\kappa}}U
are equivalent. The types are equivalent if both bound variables
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\alpha}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\beta}}
are renamed to the same variable

{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\upsilon_{j}}}
. In summary, renaming provides a syntax-guided approach to the equivalence of lambda-abstractions, where the names of bound variables should not matter. Our notion of type equivalence preserves alpha-conversions up to renaming: if
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}
,
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U}}
are alpha-conversions of one another, then
\operatorname{rename}({\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}})=\operatorname{rename}({\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U}})
and in particular
{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\operatorname{rename}(}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{)}}}}}\;{\sim}\;{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\operatorname{rename}(}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{)}}}}}
. We will come back to this point after we define type equivalence in
Section

4
.

We can easily see that renaming uses the minimum amount of variable names
possible; for example,

\operatorname{rename}({\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\lambda{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\alpha}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\colon}}{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{\textsc{t}}}}}.{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\lambda{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\beta}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\colon}}{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{\textsc{s}}}}}.{\beta}}}}}})={\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\lambda{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\upsilon_{1}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\colon}}{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{\textsc{t}}}}}.{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\lambda{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\upsilon_{1}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\colon}}{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{\textsc{s}}}}}.{\upsilon_{1}}}}}}}
. Notice
how both bound variables
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\alpha}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\beta}}
are renamed to

{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\upsilon_{1}}}
, the first variable available for replacement.
Also, renaming blatantly violates the Barendregt’s variable
convention
[
11
]
used in so many works; for example

\operatorname{rename}({\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\upsilon_{1}}\,{({\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\lambda{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\alpha}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\colon}}{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{T}}.{\alpha}}})}}})={\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\upsilon_{1}}\,{({\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\lambda{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\upsilon_{1}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\colon}}{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{T}}.{\upsilon_{1}}}})}}}
, where variable

{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\upsilon_{1}}}
is both free and bound in the resulting type.
Even if renaming violates the variable convention, substitution can still be
performed without resorting to the “on-the-fly” renaming of Curry and
Feys
[
22
,
40
]
. When
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\upsilon_{1}}}\neq{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\upsilon_{2}}}
,
we have

{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{({\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\lambda{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\upsilon_{1}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\colon}}{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{\kappa}}}}.{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\lambda{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\upsilon_{2}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\colon}}{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{\kappa}}^{\prime}}}.{U}}}}}})}\,{T}}}\longrightarrow({\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\lambda{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\upsilon_{2}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\colon}}{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{\kappa}}^{\prime}}}.{U}}}){\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{[}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{/}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\upsilon_{1}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{]}}={\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\lambda{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\upsilon_{2}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\colon}}{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{\kappa}}^{\prime}}}.{(U{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{[}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{/}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\upsilon_{1}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{]}})}}}

since the renaming rule for application guarantees that

{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\upsilon_{2}}}\notin\operatorname{fv}({\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}})
.
Otherwise if
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\upsilon_{1}}}={\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\upsilon_{2}}}
, we have
({\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\lambda{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\upsilon_{1}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\colon}}{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{\kappa}}^{\prime}}}.{U}}}){\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{[}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{/}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\upsilon_{1}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{]}}={\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\lambda{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\upsilon_{1}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\colon}}{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{\kappa}}^{\prime}}}.{U}}}
.
This justifies the inclusion of set
S
in the
renaming process.
From now on, we assume that all types have gone through the renaming process.

Type reduction
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}\longrightarrow{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}

Figure 6
:
Type reduction.

Next comes the notion of
type reduction
(
Fig.

6
). Apart
from beta reduction (rule
R-
\beta
), the definition provides for
sequential composition, for unfolding recursive types and for reducing

{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{Dual}}}}\,{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}}}}
types. Note that renaming is further invoked in rule
R-
\beta
for beta reduction does not preserve renaming: consider the renamed type

{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{({\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\lambda{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\upsilon_{1}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\colon}}{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{\textsc{t}}}}}.{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\lambda{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\upsilon_{2}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\colon}}{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{\textsc{t}}}}}.{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\upsilon_{1}\rightarrow\upsilon_{2}}}}}}}}})}\,{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{Unit}}}}}}
.
The type resulting from the substitution

({\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\lambda{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\upsilon_{2}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\colon}}{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{\textsc{t}}}}}.{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\upsilon_{1}\rightarrow\upsilon_{2}}}}}}){\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{[}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{Unit}}}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{/}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\upsilon_{1}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{]}}

is
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\lambda{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\upsilon_{2}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\colon}}{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{\textsc{t}}}}}.{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{Unit}}}\rightarrow\upsilon_{2}}}}}}
which is not
renamed and, therefore, not equivalent to

{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\lambda{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\upsilon_{1}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\colon}}{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{\textsc{t}}}}}.{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{Unit}}}\rightarrow\upsilon_{1}}}}}}
according to our
rules in
Section

4
.
Thanks to our modified rule
R-
\beta
, we preserve preservation of renaming under reductions: if
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}=\operatorname{rename}({\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}})
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}\longrightarrow{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U}}
then
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U}}=\operatorname{rename}({\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U}})
.

Weak head normal form
{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}}\>\mathsf{whnf}

\frac{}{{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\iota}}}\>\mathsf{whnf}}\quad\textsc{\text{\small\sc W-{Const0}} }\par\frac{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\iota}}\neq{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{;}}\ ,{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\mu}}_{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{\kappa}}}}\ ,\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{Dual}}}\quad\;m\geq 1}{{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\iota}\,{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}_{1}\ldots{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}_{m}}}}}}}\>\mathsf{whnf}}\quad\textsc{\text{\small\sc W-{Const1}} }\par\frac{}{{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{;}\,{T}}}}}}\>\mathsf{whnf}}\quad\textsc{\text{\small\sc W-{Seq1}} }\par\frac{{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T_{1}}}}\>\mathsf{whnf}\quad{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T_{1}}}\neq{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{U};{V}}}\ ,\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{Skip}}}\quad m\geq 2}{{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{;}}}\,{T_{1}\ldots T_{m}}}}}}}\>\mathsf{whnf}}\quad\textsc{\text{\small\sc W-{Seq2}} }\par\frac{m\geq 0}{{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\alpha}\,{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}_{1}\ldots{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}_{m}}}}}}}\>\mathsf{whnf}}\quad\textsc{\text{\small\sc W-{Var}} }\par\frac{}{{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\char 12\relax{\alpha}{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{\kappa}}}{T}}}}\>\mathsf{whnf}}\quad\textsc{\text{\small\sc W-{Abs}} }\par\frac{{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T_{1}}}}\>\mathsf{whnf}\quad{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T_{1}}}\neq\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{Skip}}}\ ,\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{End}}}\ ,{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\sharp}\,{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U}}}}}\ ,{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{U};{V}}}\ ,{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\odot\{\overline{l_{i}:U_{i}}\}}}\ ,{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{Dual}}}}\,{(\alpha\ U_{1}\ldots U_{n})}}}\quad m\geq 2}{{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{Dual}}}}\,{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}_{1}\ldots{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}_{m}}}}}}}\>\mathsf{whnf}}\quad\textsc{\text{\small\sc W-{Dual}} }

Figure 7
:
Weak head normal form.

We also need the notion of
weak head normal form
,
{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}}\>\mathsf{whnf}
, borrowed from the lambda calculus
[
11
,
12
]
.
Fig.

7
provides a rule-based characterisation (which can be used in a compiler).
Notice that in rules
W-Seq1
and
W-Seq2
, the sequential composition operator
;
appears in prefix notation. However, for type formation (kinding), it turns out that
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{;}}}\,{T_{1}\ldots T_{m}}}}
is a (well formed) type only when
m\leq 2
.
The following result shows that the rule-based characterization is equivalent to irreducibility.

Lemma 1

{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}}\>\mathsf{whnf}
iff
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}\mathchoice{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 3.75pt\kern-5.27776pt$\displaystyle\not$\hss}{\longrightarrow}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 3.75pt\kern-5.27776pt$\textstyle\not$\hss}{\longrightarrow}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 2.625pt\kern-4.45831pt$\scriptstyle\not$\hss}{\longrightarrow}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 1.875pt\kern-3.95834pt$\scriptscriptstyle\not$\hss}{\longrightarrow}}}
.

Proof

(
\Rightarrow
)
Assume that
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}}}\longrightarrow{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U}}}}
for some
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U}}
. We prove that it is not the case that
{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}}}}\>\mathsf{whnf}
.

•

{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{Skip}}}};{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}}}}}}\longrightarrow{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}}}
and by inspection of
{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{}}}\>\mathsf{whnf}
rules, we conclude that
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{Skip}}}};{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}}}}
is not a weak head normal form.

•

{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}};{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U}}}}}}}\longrightarrow{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{V};{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U}}}}}}}
only if
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}}}\longrightarrow{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{V}}
. However, by hypothesis, given that
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}
reduces,
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}
is not a weak head normal form. Therefore, none of the
{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{}}}\>\mathsf{whnf}
rules can be applied. So,
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}};{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U}}}}}
is not a weak head normal form.

•

{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{({\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}};{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U}}}}})};{V}}}}}\longrightarrow{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{T};{({\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{U};{V}}})}}}}}
and by inspection of
{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{}}}\>\mathsf{whnf}
rules, we conclude that
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{({\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}};{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U}}}}})};{V}}}
is not a weak head normal form.

•

{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\mu}}_{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{\kappa}}}}}\,{T}}}}}\longrightarrow{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{T}\,{({\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\mu}}_{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{\kappa}}}}}\,{T}}})}}}}}
and by inspection of
{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{}}}\>\mathsf{whnf}
rules, we conclude that
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\mu}}_{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{k}}}\,{T}}}
is not a weak head normal form.

•

{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{(\char 12\relax{\alpha}{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{\kappa}}}{T})}\,{U}}}}}\longrightarrow{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\operatorname{rename}(}}T{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{[}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{/}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\alpha}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{]}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{)}}}}
and by inspection of
{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{}}}\>\mathsf{whnf}
rules, we conclude that
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{(\char 12\relax{\alpha}{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{\kappa}}}{T})}\,{U}}}
is not a weak head normal form.

•

{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T\ V}}\longrightarrow{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U\ V}}
only if
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}\longrightarrow{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U}}
.
However, by hypothesis, given that
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}
reduces,
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}
is not a weak head normal form. Therefore, by inspection of
{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{}}}\>\mathsf{whnf}
rules, we conclude that
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{T}\,{V}}}
is not a weak head normal form.

•

Cases of
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}
for which
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{Dual}}}}\,{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}}}}}}\longrightarrow{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U}}}}
for some
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U}}
are automatically excluded in rule
W-Dual
, therefore none of those
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{Dual}}}}\,{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}}}}
types is a weak head normal form.

(
\Leftarrow
) We must investigate all types
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}
such that
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}\mathchoice{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 3.75pt\kern-5.27776pt$\displaystyle\not$\hss}{\longrightarrow}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 3.75pt\kern-5.27776pt$\textstyle\not$\hss}{\longrightarrow}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 2.625pt\kern-4.45831pt$\scriptstyle\not$\hss}{\longrightarrow}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 1.875pt\kern-3.95834pt$\scriptscriptstyle\not$\hss}{\longrightarrow}}}
. We illustrate with a couple of cases:

•

No constant
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\iota}}
reduces, and according to
W-Const0
, they are all in weak head normal form. Analogously for
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{;{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}}}
,
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\alpha}\,{T_{1},\ldots,T_{m}}}}(m\geq 0)
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\lambda{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\alpha}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\colon}}{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{\kappa}}}}.{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}}}}
, using rules
W-Seq1
,
W-Var
and
W-Abs
, respectively.

•

The cases of
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}
for which
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}};{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U}}}}}
does not reduce are, according to rule
R-Seq2
, those where
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}
does not reduce either. By induction,
{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}}}}\>\mathsf{whnf}
. Also,
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}\neq\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{Skip}}},{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{T_{1}};{T_{2}}}}
, otherwise the type
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}};{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U}}}}}
would reduce via
R-Seq1
or
R-Assoc
, respectively. This case is covered in
W-Seq2
and therefore the type is in weak head normal form.

•

The cases of
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}
for which
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{Dual}}}(T)}}
does not reduce are all such that
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}\neq\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{Skip}}}
,
\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{End}}}
,
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{T_{1}};{T_{2}}}}
,
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\sharp{T}}}
,
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\odot\{\overline{t_{i}:T_{i}}\}}}
,
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{Dual}}}({\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\alpha}\,{T_{1}\ldots T_{m}}}})}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}
does not reduce. By induction,
{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}}}}\>\mathsf{whnf}
. Each of those cases for
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}
is covered by
W-Dual
.

We say that type
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}

normalises
to type
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U}}
, written
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}\Downarrow{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U}}
, if
{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U}}}}}\>\mathsf{whnf}
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U}}
is reached from
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}
in a finite number of
reduction steps (note that any term which is already whnf normalises to itself). We write
{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}}\>\mathsf{norm}
to denote that
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}\Downarrow{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U}}
for some
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U}}
.

For example, suppose we want to normalise the type
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\mu}}_{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{\textsc{s}}}}}}\,{T}}}
, where

{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}
is the type

\char 12\relax{\upsilon_{1}}{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{\textsc{s}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\hskip-0.43057pt\oplus\hskip-0.43057pt}}\{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{Done}}}\colon\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{End}}},\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{More}}}\colon{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{!{\alpha}}}\}};{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{Dual}}}}\,{\upsilon_{1}}}}}}}}
.
By computing all reductions from
{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\mu}}_{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{\textsc{s}}}}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}}
, we obtain the sequence

{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\mu}}_{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{\textsc{s}}}}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}}\longrightarrow{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}}\,{({\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\mu}}_{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{\textsc{s}}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}})}}}\longrightarrow{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\hskip-0.43057pt\oplus\hskip-0.43057pt}}\{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{Done}}}\colon\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{End}}},\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{More}}}\colon{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{!{\alpha}}}\}};{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{Dual}}}}\,{(}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\mu}}_{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{\textsc{s}}}}}T)}}}\mathchoice{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 3.75pt\kern-5.27776pt$\displaystyle\not$\hss}{\longrightarrow}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 3.75pt\kern-5.27776pt$\textstyle\not$\hss}{\longrightarrow}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 2.625pt\kern-4.45831pt$\scriptstyle\not$\hss}{\longrightarrow}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 1.875pt\kern-3.95834pt$\scriptscriptstyle\not$\hss}{\longrightarrow}}}

Finally, we can show that
{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\hskip-0.43057pt\oplus\hskip-0.43057pt}}\{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{Done}}}\colon\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{End}}},\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{More}}}\colon{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{!{\alpha}}}\}};{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{Dual}}}}\,{(}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\mu}}_{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{\textsc{s}}}}}T)}}}}}}\>\mathsf{whnf}
:

\frac{\frac{}{{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\hskip-0.3014pt\oplus\hskip-0.3014pt}}\{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{Done}}}\colon\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{End}}},\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{More}}}\colon{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{!{\alpha}}}\}}}}\>\mathsf{whnf}}\quad\textsc{right= \text{\small\sc W-{Const1}} }}{{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\hskip-0.43057pt\oplus\hskip-0.43057pt}}\{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{Done}}}\colon\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{End}}},\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{More}}}\colon{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{!{\alpha}}}\}};{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{Dual}}}}\,{(}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\mu}}_{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{\textsc{s}}}}}T)}}}}}}\>\mathsf{whnf}}\quad\textsc{right= \text{\small\sc W-{Seq2}} }

Hence, we conclude that
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\mu}}_{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{\textsc{s}}}}}}\,{T}}}}}\Downarrow{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\hskip-0.43057pt\oplus\hskip-0.43057pt}}\{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{Done}}}\colon\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{End}}},\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{More}}}\colon{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{!{\alpha}}}\}};{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{Dual}}}}\,{(}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\mu}}_{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{\textsc{s}}}}}T)}}}}}
.
Similarly, we can reason that

{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\mu}}_{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{\textsc{t}}}}}}\,{({\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\lambda{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\upsilon_{1}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\colon}}{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{\textsc{t}}}}}.{{\upsilon_{1}}}}})}}}
,

{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\mu}}_{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{\textsc{s}}}}}}\,{({\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\lambda{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\upsilon_{1}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\colon}}{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{\textsc{s}}}}}.{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{Skip}}}};{\upsilon_{1}}}}}}})}}}

and

{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\mu}}_{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{\textsc{s}}}}}}\,{({\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\lambda{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\upsilon_{1}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\colon}}{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{\textsc{s}}}}}.{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{Dual}}}}\,{\upsilon_{1}}}}}}})}}}

are all examples of non-normalising expressions.

Type formation
{\Delta}\vdash{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}}\;{:}\;{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{\kappa}}}}}

\frac{}{{\Delta}\vdash{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\iota}}}\;{:}\;{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{\kappa}}_{\iota}}}}}\quad\textsc{\text{\small\sc K-{Const}} }\quad\frac{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\alpha}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\colon}}{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{\kappa}}}}\in{\Delta}}{{\Delta}\vdash{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\alpha}}}\;{:}\;{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{\kappa}}}}}}\quad\textsc{\text{\small\sc K-{Var}} }\quad\frac{{\Delta+{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\alpha}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\colon}}{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{\kappa}}}}}\vdash{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}}\;{:}\;{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{\kappa}}^{\prime}}}}}{{\Delta}\vdash{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\char 12\relax\alpha{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{\kappa}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}}}}\;{:}\;{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{\kappa}}}\Rightarrow{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{\kappa}}^{\prime}}}}}}}}\quad\textsc{\text{\small\sc K-{TAbs}} }\quad\frac{{\Delta}\vdash{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}}\;{:}\;{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{\kappa}}}\Rightarrow{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{\kappa}}^{\prime}}}}}}}\;\;\,{\Delta}\vdash{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U}}}\;{:}\;{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{\kappa}}}}}\;\;\,{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{T}\,{U}}}}}}\>\mathsf{norm}}{{\Delta}\vdash{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{T}\,{U}}}}}}\;{:}\;{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{\kappa}}^{\prime}}}}}\quad\textsc{\text{\small\sc K-{TApp}} }

Figure 8
:
Type formation.

Equipped with normalisation, we can introduce
type formation
, which we do
via the rules in
Fig.

8
. Rule
K-Const
introduces constants as
types whose kinds match those of
Fig.

4
. Rule
K-Var
reads the kind
of a type variable from context
\Delta
.
An abstraction
\char 12\relax{\alpha}{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{\kappa}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}}
is a well-formed type with kind

{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{\kappa}}}\Rightarrow{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{\kappa}}^{\prime}}}}
if
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}
is well formed in context
\Delta
updated
with entry
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\alpha}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\colon}}{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{\kappa}}}}
(rule
K-TAbs
). The update is necessary since
we are dealing with renamed types and the same type variable may appear with
different kinds in nested abstractions.

It is not until we reach rule
K-TApp
that we find a proviso about the normalisation of a type.
This is standard and analogous to a condition on
contractivity.
The goal is to eliminate types that reduce indefinitely without reaching a weak head normal form.

Theorem 3.1

Let
{\Delta}\vdash{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}}\;{:}\;{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{\kappa}}}}}
.

Preservation.

If
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}\longrightarrow{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U}}
, then
{\Delta}\vdash{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U}}}\;{:}\;{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{\kappa}}}}}
.

Confluence.

If
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}\longrightarrow{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}\longrightarrow{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{V}}
, then

{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U}}\longrightarrow^{*}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{W}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{V}}\longrightarrow^{*}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{W}}
.

Weak normalisation.

{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}\Downarrow{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U}}
for some
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U}}
. Furthermore,
if
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}\Downarrow{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{V}}
, then
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U}}={\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{V}}
.

Proof

Preservation:
By rule induction on
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}\longrightarrow{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U}}
. Let us inspect some cases of (well-formed) types
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}
that reduce as an illustration:

•

{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{Skip}}}};{U}}}}}\longrightarrow{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U}}
; from the assumption that
{\Delta}\vdash{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}}}}\;{:}\;{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{\kappa}}}}}
follows that
{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{\kappa}}={\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{\textsc{s}}}
and that
{\Delta}\vdash{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U}}}}}\;{:}\;{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{\textsc{s}}}}}}
.

•

{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{U_{1}};{U_{2}}}}}}\longrightarrow{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{V};{U_{2}}}}}}
only if
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U_{1}}}\longrightarrow{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{V}}
; from the assumption that
{\Delta}\vdash{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}}}}\;{:}\;{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{\kappa}}}}}
follows that
{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{\kappa}}={\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{\textsc{s}}}
,
{\Delta}\vdash{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U}}_{1}}}}\;{:}\;{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{\textsc{s}}}}}}
and
{\Delta}\vdash{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U}}_{2}}}}\;{:}\;{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{\textsc{s}}}}}}
. By hypothesis,
{\Delta}\vdash{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{V}}}\;{:}\;{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{\textsc{s}}}}}}
. Therefore,
{\Delta}\vdash{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{V};{U_{2}}}}}}}\;{:}\;{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{\textsc{s}}}}}}
.

•

{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\mu}}_{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{k}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U}}}}\longrightarrow{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U}}}\,{({\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\mu}}_{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{k}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U}})}}}}}
; from the assumption that
{\Delta}\vdash{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}}}}\;{:}\;{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{\kappa}}}}}
and since
{\Delta}\vdash{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\mu}}_{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{\kappa}}}}}}}\;{:}\;{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{{({\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{\kappa}}}\Rightarrow{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{\kappa}}}}})}\Rightarrow{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{\kappa}}}}}}}}
, follows that
{\Delta}\vdash{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U}}}}}\;{:}\;{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{\kappa}}}\Rightarrow{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{\kappa}}}}}}}}
. Therefore,
{\Delta}\vdash{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U}}}\,{({\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\mu}}_{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{\kappa}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U}})}}}}}}\;{:}\;{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{\kappa}}}}}
.

Confluence:
By a case analysis on the various possible reductions
from
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}
. We sketch a simple case.

{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{({\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{Skip}}}};{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}}}});{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U}}}}\longrightarrow{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{T};{U}}}}}
(via
R-Seq2
) and

{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{({\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{Skip}}}};{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}}}});{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U}}}}\longrightarrow{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{Skip}}};({\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{T};{U}}})}}
(via
R-Assoc
).
Nonetheless, the latter reduces again via
R-Seq1
:

{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{Skip}}};({\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{T};{U}}})}}\longrightarrow{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{T};{U}}}}}
.

Weak normalisation:
By a case analysis on
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}
. If
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}={\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\iota}},{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\alpha}}
or
\char 12\relax\alpha{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{\kappa}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U}}
, then
{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}}}}\>\mathsf{whnf}
by rules
\text{\small\sc W-{Const0}},\text{\small\sc W-{Var}},\text{\small\sc W-{Abs}}
, respectively. Therefore
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}}}\Downarrow{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}}}
according to
N-Whnf
. If
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}={\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{U}\,{V}}}
, then according to
K-TApp

{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{UV}}}\>\mathsf{norm}
, so
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}\Downarrow{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T^{\prime}}}
for some
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T^{\prime}}}
by definition.
The second part follows from confluence.

We finally arrive at the main decidability result in this section. In its proof, we make use of the fact that recursion is restricted to kind

{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{*}}
to limit the possible subexpressions of the form

{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\mu}}_{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{*}}}}}\,{U}}}
that might appear in the normalisation of
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}
.

Theorem 3.2
(Decidability of type formation)

{\Delta}\vdash{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}}}}\;{:}\;{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{\kappa}}}}}
is decidable for types in
F^{\mu_{\ast};}_{\omega}
.

Proof

In
Appendix

0.A
.

4
Type equivalence

This section introduces type bisimulation as our notion of type equivalence.
We define a labelled transition system (LTS) on the space of all types and write

{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}{a}}}}{{\longrightarrow}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U}}
to denote that
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}
has a transition by label
{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}{a}}
to

{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U}}
. The grammar for labels and the LTS rules are in
Fig.

9
.

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}{a}}

\displaystyle\;::=\;{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}{\alpha_{i}}}\;\mid\;{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}{\iota_{i}}}\;\mid\;{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}{\lambda\alpha\colon{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{\kappa}}}}

\displaystyle(i\geq 0,{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}{\iota}}\neq\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{Skip}}})

Figure 9
:
Labelled transition system for types.

If
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}
is not in weak head normal form, then we must normalise it to some type
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U}}
, so that
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}
has the same transitions as
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U}}
(rule
L-Red
).
Otherwise if
{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}}\>\mathsf{whnf}
, then the transitions of
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}
can be immediately derived by looking at the corresponding rule for
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}
as follows.
If
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}
is a variable, use rule
L-Var1
(with
m=0
).
If
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}
is a constant (other than
\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{Skip}}}
), use rule
L-Const
.
Note that if
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}
is a lone
\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{Skip}}}
, it has no transitions.
If
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}
is an abstraction, use rule
L-Abs
.

If
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}
is an application, then we need to look inside the head. We write
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}

as
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T_{0}\ T_{1}\ldots T_{m}}}
with
m\geq 1
where
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T_{0}}}
is not an
application, and look at
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T_{0}}}
. If
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T_{0}}}
is a variable, use
rules
L-Var1
and
L-Var2
. If
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T_{0}}}
is one of the
constants
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\rightarrow}}
,
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\forall}}_{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{\kappa}}
,
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\odot\{\overline{l_{i}}\}}}
or

{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\llparenthesis{\overline{l_{i}}}\rrparenthesis}}
, use rule
L-ConstApp
. Note that if
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T_{0}}}

is an abstraction or
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\mu}}_{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{\kappa}}}}
, then
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}
is not in weak head normal form and
it must be normalised. If
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T_{0}}}
is
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\sharp}}
, we use rules

L-Msg1
and
L-Msg2
. If
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T_{0}}}
is
\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{Dual}}}
, then the
only way for
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}
to be well-formed and in weak head normal form is if
m=1

and
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T_{1}}}
is
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\alpha}}
or
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\alpha\ U_{1}\ldots U_{m}}}
, in which case
we use rules
L-DualVar1
and
L-DualVar2
.

If
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T_{0}}}
is
;
, we require an additional case analysis on

{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T_{1}}}
. If
m=1
, use rule
L-Seq1
. Otherwise
m=2
due to
kinding. If
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T_{1}}}
is a variable, use rule
L-VarSeq1
(with

m=0
). If
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T_{1}}}
is a constant, then it must be of kind
s
, so it
must be either
\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{Skip}}}
or
\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{End}}}
. If
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T_{1}}}
is
\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{Skip}}}
, then
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}
is not
in weak normal form and it must be normalised. If
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T_{1}}}
is
\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{End}}}
, use
rule
L-EndSeq
(
\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{End}}}
is an absorbing element, so
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{End}}}};{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U}}}}}
simply makes a transition to
\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{Skip}}}
without executing
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U}}
). Note that
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T_{1}}}
cannot be an abstraction due
to kinding.

If
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T_{1}}}
is an application, then again we write
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T_{1}}}
as
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U_{0}\ U_{1}\ldots U_{n}}}
with
n\geq 1
where the head
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U_{0}}}
is not an application, and look at
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U_{0}}}
.
If
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U_{0}}}
is a variable, use rules
L-VarSeq1
and
L-VarSeq2
.
If
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U_{0}}}
is a constant, it must be one of
;
,
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\mu}}_{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{\kappa}}}}
,
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\sharp}}
,
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\odot\{\overline{l_{i}}\}}}
or
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{Dual}}}}}
due to kinding.
If
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U_{0}}}
is
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\sharp}}
, use rules
L-MsgSeq1
and
L-MsgSeq2
.
If
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U_{0}}}
is
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\odot\{\overline{l_{i}}\}}}
, use rule
L-ChoiceSeq
.
If
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U_{0}}}
is
\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{Dual}}}
, the only way for
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}
to be well-formed and in weak head normal form is if
n=1
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U_{1}}}
is
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\alpha}}
or
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\alpha\ V_{1}\ldots V_{\ell}}}
, in which case we use rules
L-DualSeq1
and
L-DualSeq2
.
Finally if
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U_{0}}}
is
;
,
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\mu}}_{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{\kappa}}}}
or an abstraction,
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}
is not in weak normal form and it must be normalised.

:
The LTS for type
\char 12\relax{\upsilon_{1}}{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{\textsc{t}}}U
. Normalisation

{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T_{1}}}\Downarrow{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T_{2}}}
is represented as
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T_{1}}}\Rightarrow{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T_{2}}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U}}
is a shorthand for type

{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\mu}}_{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{\textsc{s}}}}}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{(}}\char 12\relax{\upsilon_{2}}{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{\textsc{s}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\hskip-0.38751pt\oplus\hskip-0.38751pt}}\{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{Done}}}\colon\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{End}}},\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{More}}}\colon{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{!{\upsilon_{1}}}}\}};{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{Dual}}}}\,{\upsilon_{2}}}}}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{)}}
.

Let us clarify our LTS rules with an example. Consider the following type

\char 12\relax{\upsilon_{1}}{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{\textsc{t}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\mu}}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\upsilon_{2}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\colon}}{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{\textsc{s}}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{.\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\hskip-0.43057pt\oplus\hskip-0.43057pt}}\{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{Done}}}\colon\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{End}}},\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{More}}}\colon{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{!{\upsilon_{1}}}}\}};{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{Dual}}}}\,{\upsilon_{2}}}}}}}}}}
and call it

{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}
. Clearly
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}
is of kind
{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{\textsc{t}}}}\Rightarrow{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{\textsc{s}}}}}}
.
For a given functional type
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\upsilon_{1}}}
, this type specifies a channel that alternates between: offer a choice and output a value of type
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\upsilon_{1}}}
; or select a choice and input a value of type
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\upsilon_{1}}}
. The polarity is swapped thanks to the application of constant
\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{Dual}}}
to the recursion variable
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\upsilon_{2}}}
.
To construct the (fragment of the) LTS generated by this type, let us first
desugar
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}
into
\char 12\relax{\upsilon_{1}}{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{\textsc{t}}}U
where
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U}}
is the type
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\mu}}_{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{\textsc{s}}}}}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{(}}\char 12\relax{\upsilon_{2}}{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{\textsc{s}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\hskip-0.43057pt\oplus\hskip-0.43057pt}}\{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{Done}}}\colon\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{End}}},\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{More}}}\colon{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{!{\upsilon_{1}}}}\}};{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{Dual}}}}\,{\upsilon_{2}}}}}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{)}}
.
Notice that
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U}}
normalises to

{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\hskip-0.43057pt\oplus\hskip-0.43057pt}}\{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{Done}}}\colon\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{End}}},\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{More}}}\colon{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{!{\upsilon_{1}}}}\}};{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{Dual}}}}\,{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U}}}}}}}}}
.
The LTS for the example is sketched in
Fig.

10
. In this case,
only finitely many types appear. However, more elaborate examples involving
sequential composition or higher-order recursion may lead to an infinite graph
of transitions.

Given the LTS rules, we can define, in the standard way, a notion of bisimulation.

A binary relation
R
on types is called a
bisimulation
if, for every

({\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}},{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U}})\in R
and every transition label
{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}{a}}
:

1.

if
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}{a}}}}{{\longrightarrow}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T^{\prime}}}
, then there exists
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U^{\prime}}}
s.t.
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}{a}}}}{{\longrightarrow}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U^{\prime}}}
and
({\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T^{\prime}}},{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U^{\prime}}})\in R
;

2.

if
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}{a}}}}{{\longrightarrow}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U^{\prime}}}
, then there exists
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T^{\prime}}}
s.t.
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}{a}}}}{{\longrightarrow}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T^{\prime}}}
and
({\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T^{\prime}}},{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U^{\prime}}})\in R
.

We say that types
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U}}
are bisimilar, written
{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}}\;{\sim}\;{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U}}}
, if there exists a bisimulation
R
such that
({\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}},{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U}})\in R
.

Intuitively, a notion of type equivalence must preserve and reflect the syntax of type constructors: for example, a type
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T\rightarrow U}}
is equivalent to a type
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T^{\prime}\rightarrow U^{\prime}}}
iff
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}
,
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T^{\prime}}}
are equivalent and
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U}}
,
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U^{\prime}}}
are equivalent.
Using the bisimulation technique, we achieve this by considering a labelled transition system on types:
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T\rightarrow U}}
has a transition labelled
{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}{\rightarrow_{1}}}
to
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}
and a transition labelled
{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}{\rightarrow_{2}}}
to
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U}}
.
In this way,
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T\rightarrow U}}
can only be equivalent to another type which has two transitions with those same labels.
For each of the type constructors (

{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\rightarrow}}
,
