{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\forall_{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{\kappa}}}}
,

{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{!}}
,
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{?}}
,
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\odot{\{\overline{\ell_{i}}\}}}}
,
and so on) we have suitable transition rules.
Moreover, a type sometimes needs to be reduced before a type constructor is found at the root of the syntax tree.
If
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}
normalizes to
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U}}
, then we expect
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U}}
to be bisimilar, which is achieved thanks to rule
L-Red
.
This handles the various reductions:
beta-reductions arising from lambda-abstraction and applications (e.g.,
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{(\char 12\relax\alpha{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{\kappa}}T)}\,{U}}}
reduces to
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{[}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U}}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{/}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\alpha}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{]}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}
),
reductions arising from the monoidal structure of sequential composition (e.g.,
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{Skip}}}};{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}}}}
reduces to
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}
),
reductions arising from the internalization of duality as a type constructor (e.g.,
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{Dual}}}}\,{({\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{!{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}}}})}}}
reduces to
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{?{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}}}}
)
and reductions arising from the recursion (e.g.,
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\mu_{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{\kappa}}}\,{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}}}}
reduces to
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}}\,{({\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\mu_{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{\kappa}}}\,{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}}}})}}}
).

Let us look at some examples of bisimilarity.

•

Consider the types
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}={\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\alpha\ (!\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{Int}}})}}
and

{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U}}={\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\alpha\ (!\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{Int}}})};{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{Skip}}}}}}
. These are bisimilar, since they both
exhibit a transition with label
{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}{\alpha_{1}}}
to
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{!{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{Int}}}}}}
(rules

L-Var2
and
L-VarSeq2
resp.) and another transition with
label
{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}{\alpha_{0}}}
to
\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{Skip}}}
(rules
L-Var1
and

L-VarSeq1
resp.). In general, two types
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\alpha}}\ {\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}
and

{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\alpha\ {\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U}}};{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{V}}}}}
are bisimilar iff
{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}}}}\;{\sim}\;{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U}}}}}
and

{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{Skip}}}}}}\;{\sim}\;{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{V}}}}}
. This justifies our choice for the rules with
variables.

•

Similar considerations apply to
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\sharp{T}}}
vs
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\sharp{T}}}};{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{Skip}}}}}}
and
to
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{Dual}}}}\,{(\alpha\ T_{1}\ldots T_{m})}}}
vs

{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{Dual}}}}\,{(\alpha\ T_{1}\ldots T_{m})}}}};{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{Skip}}}}}}
.

•

For internal and external choices, the situation is slightly different.
Types
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\odot\{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{Go}}}\colon{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{T};{V}}},\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{Quit}}}\colon{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{U};{V}}}\}}}
and

{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\odot\{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{Go}}}\colon T,\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{Quit}}}\colon U\}};{V}}}
are bisimilar. The
former exhibits transitions
{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}{\odot\{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}{Go}}},\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}{Quit}}}\}_{1}}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}{\odot\{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}{Go}}},\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}{Quit}}}\}_{2}}}
to

{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{T};{V}}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{U};{V}}}
resp., due to rule
L-ConstApp
. The
latter exhibits the same transitions, but due to rule
L-ChoiceSeq

instead. This is as desired (distributivity of sequential composition against
choice) and justifies our choice for those rules.

•

\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{End}}}
is bisimilar to any type
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{End}}}};{U}}}
, due to our choice of rules
L-Const
and
L-EndSeq
.

•

\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{Skip}}}
is the only well-formed type in weak head normal form that has no transitions.
Hence, if
{\Delta}\vdash{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}}\;{:}\;{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{\kappa}}}}}
, we have
{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}}\;{\sim}\;{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{Skip}}}}}}
iff
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}\Downarrow{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{Skip}}}}}
.

•

Consider the types
\char 12\relax\alpha{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{\kappa}}T
and
\char 12\relax\beta{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{\kappa}}U
.
Before generating the LTS, we must rename them to
\char 12\relax{\upsilon_{i}}{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{\kappa}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{[}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\upsilon_{i}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{/}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\alpha}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{]}}T}
and
\char 12\relax{\upsilon_{j}}{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{\kappa}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{[}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\upsilon_{j}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{/}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\beta}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{]}}U}
.
According to rule
L-Abs
, the renamed types are bisimilar only if
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\upsilon_{i}}}={\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\upsilon_{j}}}
, in which case we check the equivalence of
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{[}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\upsilon_{i}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{/}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\alpha}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{]}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{[}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\upsilon_{j}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{/}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\beta}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{]}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U}}
.
Here, the choice of
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\upsilon_{i}}}
depends only on
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}
(not on
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U}}
).
This allows us to define an LTS for
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}
(independently of
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U}}
), and an LTS for
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U}}
(independently of
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}
), in a way that the types are equivalent iff the corresponding LTS are bisimilar.
Without using renaming, we would have to somehow “remember” that variables
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\alpha}}
,
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\beta}}
should be “linked”, which would make the generation of independent LTS impossible.
This is the main technical issue solved by our notion of renaming.

Our notion of type equivalence enjoys natural properties and behaves as expected with respect to the notions of reduction, normalisation and kinding from
Section

3
.
We can derive rules for type equivalence, that could be used to define another coinductive notion of equivalence, via effective syntax-directed rules.
In other words, one could prove that
\sim
is the greatest relation satisfying properties 1-15 below.

Lemma 2

1.

{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}}\;{\sim}\;{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\alpha}}}
iff
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}\Downarrow{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\alpha}}
.

2.

{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}}\;{\sim}\;{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\char 12\relax\alpha{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{\kappa}}U}}}
iff
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}\Downarrow{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\char 12\relax\alpha{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{\kappa}}V}}
for some
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{V}}
s.t.
{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U}}}\;{\sim}\;{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{V}}}
.

3.

{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}}\;{\sim}\;{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{End}}}}}}
iff

•

{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}\Downarrow{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{End}}}}}
; or

•

{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}\Downarrow{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{End}}}};{U}}}}}
for some
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U}}
.

4.

Let
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\iota}}
be a constant other than
\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{End}}}
. Then
{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}}\;{\sim}\;{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\iota}}}
iff
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}\Downarrow{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\iota}}
.

5.

{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}}\;{\sim}\;{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\alpha\ U_{1}\ldots U_{m}}}}
iff:

•

{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}\Downarrow{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\alpha\ U^{\prime}_{1}\ldots U^{\prime}_{m}}}
for some
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U^{\prime}_{1}}},\ldots,{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U^{\prime}_{m}}}
s.t.
{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U_{j}}}}\;{\sim}\;{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U^{\prime}_{j}}}}
for each
j
; or

•

{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}\Downarrow{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{(\alpha\ U^{\prime}_{1}\ldots U^{\prime}_{m})};{W}}}}}
for some
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U^{\prime}_{1}}},\ldots,{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U^{\prime}_{m}}},{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{V}}
s.t.
{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U_{j}}}}\;{\sim}\;{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U^{\prime}_{j}}}}
and
{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{Skip}}}}}}\;{\sim}\;{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{V}}}
.

6.

{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}}\;{\sim}\;{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U\rightarrow V}}}}}
iff
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}\Downarrow{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U^{\prime}\rightarrow V^{\prime}}}}}
for some
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U^{\prime}}},{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{V^{\prime}}}
s.t.
{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U}}}\;{\sim}\;{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U^{\prime}}}}
and
{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{V}}}\;{\sim}\;{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{V^{\prime}}}}
.

7.

{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}}\;{\sim}\;{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\forall_{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{\kappa}}U}}}
iff
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}\Downarrow{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\forall_{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{\kappa}}U^{\prime}}}
for some
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U^{\prime}}}
s.t.
{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U}}}\;{\sim}\;{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U^{\prime}}}}
.

8.

{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}}\;{\sim}\;{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\llparenthesis{\ell}\colon{U_{\ell}}\rrparenthesis_{{\ell}\in{L}}}}}
iff
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}\Downarrow{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\llparenthesis{\ell}\colon{U^{\prime}_{\ell}}\rrparenthesis_{{\ell}\in{L}}}}
for some
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U^{\prime}_{\ell}}}
s.t.
{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U_{\ell}}}}\;{\sim}\;{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U^{\prime}_{\ell}}}}
for each
\ell
.

9.

{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}}\;{\sim}\;{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\sharp{U}}}}}}
iff:

•

{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}\Downarrow{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\sharp{U^{\prime}}}}}}
for some
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U^{\prime}}}
s.t.
{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U}}}\;{\sim}\;{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U^{\prime}}}}
; or

•

{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}\Downarrow{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\sharp{U^{\prime}}}}};{V}}}}}
for some
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U^{\prime}}},{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{V}}
s.t.
{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U}}}\;{\sim}\;{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U^{\prime}}}}
and
{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{Skip}}}}}}\;{\sim}\;{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{V}}}
.

10.

{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}}\;{\sim}\;{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\odot\{{\ell}\colon{U_{\ell}}\}_{{\ell}\in{L}}}}}
iff:

•

{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}\Downarrow{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\odot\{{\ell}\colon{U^{\prime}_{\ell}}\}_{{\ell}\in{L}}}}
for some
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U^{\prime}_{\ell}}}
s.t.
{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U_{\ell}}}}\;{\sim}\;{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U^{\prime}_{\ell}}}}
for each
\ell
; or

•

{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}\Downarrow{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\odot\{{\ell}\colon{U^{\prime}_{\ell}}\}_{{\ell}\in{L}}};{V}}}}}
for some
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U^{\prime}_{\ell}}},{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{V}}
s.t.
{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U_{\ell}}}}\;{\sim}\;{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{U^{\prime}_{\ell}};{V}}}}}}
for each
\ell
.

11.

{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}}\;{\sim}\;{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{End}}}};{U}}}}}}
iff

•

{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}\Downarrow{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{End}}}}}
; or

•

{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}\Downarrow{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{End}}}};{U}}}^{\prime}}}
for some
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U^{\prime}}}
.

12.

{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}}\;{\sim}\;{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\sharp{U}}}};{V}}}}}}
iff:

•

{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}\Downarrow{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\sharp{U^{\prime}}}}}}
for some
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U^{\prime}}}
s.t.
{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U}}}\;{\sim}\;{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U^{\prime}}}}
and
{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{V}}}\;{\sim}\;{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{Skip}}}}}}
; or

•

{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}\Downarrow{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\sharp{U^{\prime}}}}};{V^{\prime}}}}}}
for some
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U^{\prime}}},{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{V^{\prime}}}
s.t.
{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U}}}\;{\sim}\;{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U^{\prime}}}}
and
{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{V}}}\;{\sim}\;{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{V^{\prime}}}}
.

13.

{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}}\;{\sim}\;{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\odot\{{\ell}\colon{U_{\ell}}\}_{{\ell}\in{L}}};{V}}}}}}
iff:

•

{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}\Downarrow{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\odot\{{\ell}\colon{U^{\prime}_{\ell}}\}_{{\ell}\in{L}}}}
for some
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U^{\prime}_{\ell}}}
s.t.
{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{U_{\ell}};{V}}}}}}\;{\sim}\;{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U^{\prime}_{\ell}}}}
for each
\ell
; or

•

{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}\Downarrow{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\odot\{{\ell}\colon{U^{\prime}_{\ell}}\}_{{\ell}\in{L}}};{V}}}^{\prime}}}
for some
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U^{\prime}}},{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{V^{\prime}}}
s.t.
{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{U_{\ell}};{V}}}}}}\;{\sim}\;{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{U^{\prime}_{\ell}};{V}}}}}}
for each
\ell
.

14.

{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}}\;{\sim}\;{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{Dual}}}}\,{(\alpha\ U_{1}\ldots U_{m})}}}}}}
iff

•

{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}\Downarrow{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{Dual}}}}\,{(\alpha\ U^{\prime}_{1}\ldots U^{\prime}_{m})}}}}}
for some
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U^{\prime}_{j}}}
s.t.
{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U_{j}}}}\;{\sim}\;{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U^{\prime}_{j}}}}
for each
j
; or

•

{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}\Downarrow{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{Dual}}}}\,{(\alpha\ U^{\prime}_{1}\ldots U^{\prime}_{m})}}}};{V}}}}}
for some
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U^{\prime}_{j}}},{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{V}}
s.t.
{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U_{j}}}}\;{\sim}\;{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U^{\prime}_{j}}}}
for each
j
and
{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{Skip}}}}}}\;{\sim}\;{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{V}}}
.

15.

{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}}\;{\sim}\;{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{Dual}}}}\,{(\alpha\ U_{1}\ldots U_{m})}}}};{V}}}}}}
iff

•

{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}\Downarrow{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{Dual}}}}\,{(\alpha\ U^{\prime}_{1}\ldots U^{\prime}_{m})}}}}}
for some
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U^{\prime}_{j}}}
s.t.
{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U_{j}}}}\;{\sim}\;{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U^{\prime}_{j}}}}
for each
j
and
{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{V}}}\;{\sim}\;{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{Skip}}}}}}
; or

•

{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}\Downarrow{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{Dual}}}}\,{(\alpha\ U^{\prime}_{1}\ldots U^{\prime}_{m})}}}};{V}}}^{\prime}}}
for some
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U^{\prime}_{j}}},{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{V^{\prime}}}
s.t.
{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U_{j}}}}\;{\sim}\;{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U^{\prime}_{j}}}}
for each
j
and
{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{V}}}\;{\sim}\;{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{V^{\prime}}}}
.

Next, we show that type equivalence is preserved under renaming, reduction and normalization.

Lemma 3

1.

If
{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}}\;{\sim}\;{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U}}}
, then
{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\operatorname{rename}(}}T{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{)}}}}}\;{\sim}\;{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\operatorname{rename}(}}U{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{)}}}}}
.

2.

If
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}\longrightarrow{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U}}
, then
{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}}\;{\sim}\;{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U}}}
. If
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}\Downarrow{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U}}
, then
{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}}\;{\sim}\;{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U}}}
.

3.

Suppose that
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}\Downarrow{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T^{\prime}}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U}}\Downarrow{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U^{\prime}}}
. Then
{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}}\;{\sim}\;{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U}}}
iff
{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T^{\prime}}}}\;{\sim}\;{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U^{\prime}}}}
.

Proof

Item

1
follows by coinduction on
{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}}\;{\sim}\;{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U}}}
, i.e., we prove that the set
\{({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\operatorname{rename}(}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{)}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\operatorname{rename}(}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{)}}):{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}}\;{\sim}\;{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U}}}\}
is a bisimulation.
The only interesting case is that in which
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}=\char 12\relax\alpha{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{\kappa}}{T^{\prime}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U}}=\char 12\relax\beta{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{\kappa^{\prime}}}{U^{\prime}}
.
Since
{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}}\;{\sim}\;{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U}}}
, we get that
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\alpha}}={\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\beta}}
,
{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{\kappa}}={\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{\kappa^{\prime}}}
and
{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T^{\prime}}}}\;{\sim}\;{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U^{\prime}}}}
.
It is straightforward to see that
{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}}\;{\sim}\;{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U}}}
implies
\operatorname{fv}({\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}})=\operatorname{fv}({\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U}})
since free variables are eventually ‘seen’ in the process of consuming
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}
,
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U}}
.
Therefore,
\operatorname{first}({\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}})=\operatorname{first}({\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U}})={\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\upsilon_{k}}}
for some
k
, and
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\operatorname{rename}(}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{)}}=\char 12\relax{\upsilon_{k}}{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{\kappa}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\operatorname{rename}(}}T^{\prime}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{[}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\upsilon_{k}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{/}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\alpha}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{]}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{)}}}
,
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\operatorname{rename}(}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{)}}=\char 12\relax{\upsilon_{k}}{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{\kappa}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\operatorname{rename}(}}U^{\prime}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{[}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\upsilon_{k}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{/}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\alpha}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{]}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{)}}}
.
Finally, we apply
L-Abs
to the pair
({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\operatorname{rename}(}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{)}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\operatorname{rename}(}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{)}})
, arriving at the pair
({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\operatorname{rename}(}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T^{\prime}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{[}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\upsilon_{k}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{/}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\alpha}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{]}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{)}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\operatorname{rename}(}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U^{\prime}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{[}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\upsilon_{k}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{/}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\alpha}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{]}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{)}})
.
This lies in our set since
{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T^{\prime}}}}\;{\sim}\;{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U^{\prime}}}}
implies
{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T^{\prime}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{[}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\upsilon_{k}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{/}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\alpha}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{]}}}}}\;{\sim}\;{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U^{\prime}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{[}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\upsilon_{k}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{/}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\alpha}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{]}}}}}
.

The first part of
Item

2
is straightforward: if
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}\longrightarrow{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U}}
, then by
L-Red
every transition
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}{a}}}}{{\longrightarrow}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{V}}
is matched by a transition
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}{a}}}}{{\longrightarrow}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{V}}
and vice-versa. To prove the second part of
Item

2
, assume that
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}\Downarrow{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U}}
and consider a finite sequence of reductions
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}={\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T_{0}}}\longrightarrow{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T_{1}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{}}\longrightarrow{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\ldots}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{}}\longrightarrow{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T_{n}}}={\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U}}
. At each step of the sequence we have
{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T_{k}}}}\;{\sim}\;{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T_{k+1}}}}
and thus (since
{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{}}}\;{\sim}\;{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{}}}
is an equivalence relation) we get
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}={{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T_{0}}}}\;{\sim}\;{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T_{n}}}}={\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U}}
.

Item

3
is a direct consequence of
Item

2
.
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}\Downarrow{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T^{\prime}}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U}}\Downarrow{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U^{\prime}}}
imply
{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}}\;{\sim}\;{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T^{\prime}}}}
and
{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U}}}\;{\sim}\;{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U^{\prime}}}}
. Since
{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{}}}\;{\sim}\;{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{}}}
is an equivalence relation, we get
{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}}\;{\sim}\;{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U}}}
iff
{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T^{\prime}}}}\;{\sim}\;{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U^{\prime}}}}
.

Finally, we show that the axioms for sequential composition in the introduction (
1
) are derivable from our notion of bisimulation.

Lemma 4

1.

{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{Skip}}}};{T}}}}}}\;{\sim}\;{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}}
(Neutral element).

2.

{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{End}}}};{T}}}}}}\;{\sim}\;{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{End}}}}}}
(Absorbing element).

3.

{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{({\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{T};{U}}})};{V}}}}}}\;{\sim}\;{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{T};{({\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{U};{V}}})}}}}}}
(Associativity).

4.

{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\odot}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\{\overline{{l_{i}}\colon{T_{i}}}\}}}};{U}}}}}}\;{\sim}\;{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\odot}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\{\overline{{l_{i}}\colon{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{T_{i}};{U}}}}}\}}}}}}
(Distributivity).

Proof

Item

1
follows from the observation that
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{Skip}}}};{T}}}}}\longrightarrow{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}
(
R-Seq1
), together with
Item

2
of
Lemma

3
.

Item

2
, follows from the observation that
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{End}}}};{T}}}}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}{\mathsf{End}}}}}{{\longrightarrow}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{Skip}}}}}
(
L-EndSeq
) and
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{End}}}}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}{\mathsf{End}}}}}{{\longrightarrow}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{Skip}}}}}
(
L-Const
).

Item

3
follows from the observation that
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{({\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{T};{U}}})};{V}}}}}\longrightarrow{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{T};{({\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{U};{V}}})}}}}}
(
R-Assoc
), together with
Item

2
of
Lemma

3
.

Item

4
follows from the observation that
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\odot}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\{\overline{{l_{i}}\colon{T_{i}}}\}}}};{U}}}}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}{\odot\{\overline{l_{i}}\}_{j}}}}}}}{{\longrightarrow}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{T_{j}};{U}}}}}
(
L-ChoiceSeq
) and
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\odot}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\{\overline{{l_{i}}\colon{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{T_{i}};{U}}}}}\}}}}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}{\odot\{\overline{l_{i}}\}_{j}}}}}}}{{\longrightarrow}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{T_{j}};{U}}}}}
(
L-ConstApp
).

5
Decidability of type equivalence

This section presents results on decidability of type equivalence. Our approach
consists in translating types to objects in some computational model. We look at
finite-state automata (for types in
F^{\mu}
,
F^{\mu_{\ast}}_{\omega}
,
F^{\mu\cdot}
, and

F^{\mu_{\ast}\cdot}_{\omega}
), simple grammars (for types in
F^{\mu;}
and

F^{\mu_{\ast};}_{\omega}
) and deterministic pushdown automata (for types in

F^{\mu}_{\omega}
,
F^{\mu\cdot}_{\omega}
and
F^{\mu;}_{\omega}
).

We say that a
grammar in Greibach normal form
is a tuple

(\mathcal{T},\mathcal{N},{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{\gamma}},\mathcal{R})
where:
\mathcal{T}
is a set of terminal symbols, denoted by

{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}{a}},{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}{b}},{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}{c}}
;
\mathcal{N}
is a set of nonterminal symbols, denoted
by
{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{X}},{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{Y}},{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{Z}}
;
{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{\gamma}}\in\mathcal{N}^{\ast}
is the
starting word;
and
\mathcal{R}\subseteq\mathcal{N}\times\mathcal{T}\times\mathcal{N}^{\ast}
is a set of productions. A
grammar is said to be
simple
if, for every nonterminal
{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{X}}
and every
terminal
{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}{a}}
, there is at most one production

({\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{X}},{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}{a}},{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{\delta}})\in\mathcal{R}

[
51
]
.

Greek letters
{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{\gamma}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{\delta}}
denote (possibly empty) words of
nonterminal symbols.
Productions are written as
{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{X}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}{a}}}}{{\longrightarrow}}{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{\delta}}
.
We define a notion of bisimulation for grammars via a
labelled transition system. The system comprises a set of states
\mathcal{N}^{\ast}

corresponding to words of nonterminal symbols. For each production

{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{X}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}{a}}}}{{\longrightarrow}}{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{\gamma}}
and each word of nonterminal symbols

{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{\delta}}
, we have a labelled transition

{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{X\delta}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}{a}}}}{{\longrightarrow}}{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{\gamma\delta}}
. We let
\approx
denote the bisimulation
relation for grammars (the definition is similar to that in
Section

4
).

For the moment we focus on the class
F^{\mu_{\ast};}_{\omega}
and we explain how to
convert a type
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}
into a simple grammar

(\mathcal{T}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}},\mathcal{N}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}},\operatorname{word}({\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}),\mathcal{R}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}})
.
The conversion is based
on a function
\operatorname{word}({\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}})
that maps each type
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}
into a word of nonterminal
symbols, while introducing fresh nonterminals and productions. In our construction, following the approach by Costa et al.
[
21
]
, we use a nonterminal symbol with no productions, denoted by
{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{\bot}}
, in order to separate the two descendants of a send/receive operation such as
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{!{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}}}}};{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U}}}}}
.
The sequence of nonterminal symbols
\operatorname{word}({\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}})
is defined as follows.
First consider the cases in which
{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}}}}\>\mathsf{whnf}
.

•

For any
m\geq 0
:

\operatorname{word}({\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\alpha}}\ {\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T_{1}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\ldots}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T_{m}}})={\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{Y}}
for
{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{Y}}
a fresh
nonterminal symbol with a production
{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{Y}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}{\alpha_{0}}}}}{{\longrightarrow}}{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{\varepsilon}}}}
as well as

{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{Y}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}{\alpha_{j}}}}}{{\longrightarrow}}{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\operatorname{word}(}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T_{j}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{)}}\bot}}
for each
1\leq j\leq m
.

•

\operatorname{word}(\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{Skip}}})={\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{\varepsilon}}
.

•

\operatorname{word}(\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{End}}})={\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{Y}}
for
{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{Y}}
a fresh symbol with a single
production
{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{Y}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}{\mathsf{End}}}}}{{\longrightarrow}}{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{\bot}}
.

•

for any
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\iota}}\neq\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{Skip}}},\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{End}}}
:
\operatorname{word}({\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\iota}})={\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{Y}}
for
{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{Y}}
a fresh
nonterminal symbol with a single production
{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{Y}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}{\iota}}}}{{\longrightarrow}}{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{\varepsilon}}}}
.

•

\operatorname{word}({\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\lambda{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\alpha}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\colon}}{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{\kappa}}}}.{T}}})={\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{Y}}
for
{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{Y}}
a fresh
symbol with a production
{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{Y}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}{\lambda\alpha\colon{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{\kappa}}}}}}{{\longrightarrow}}{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\operatorname{word}(}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{)}}}}
.

•

for any
m\geq 1
and for
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\iota}}
one of
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\rightarrow}}
,

{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\forall}}_{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{\kappa}}}}
,
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\odot}}\{\overline{l_{i}}\}}}
,
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\llparenthesis{\overline{l_{i}}}\rrparenthesis}}
:

\operatorname{word}({\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\iota\ T_{1}\cdots T_{m}}})={\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{Y}}
for a fresh nonterminal

{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{Y}}
with a production
{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{Y}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}{\iota_{j}}}}}{{\longrightarrow}}{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\operatorname{word}(}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T_{j}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{)}}}}
for each

1\leq j\leq m
.

•

\operatorname{word}({\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\sharp{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}}}})={\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{Y}}
for
{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{Y}}
a fresh symbol with productions
{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{Y}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}{\sharp_{1}}}}}}}{{\longrightarrow}}{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\operatorname{word}(}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{)}}\bot}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{Y}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}{\sharp_{2}}}}}{{\longrightarrow}}{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{\varepsilon}}}}
.

•

\operatorname{word}({\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{;}}\ {\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}})={\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{Y}}
for
{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{Y}}
a fresh symbol with a production
{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{Y}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}{;_{1}}}}}{{\longrightarrow}}{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\operatorname{word}(}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{)}}}}
.

•

\operatorname{word}({\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}};{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U}}}}})=\operatorname{word}({\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}})\operatorname{word}({\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U}})
.

•

\operatorname{word}({\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{Dual}}}}\,{({\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\alpha}}\ {\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T_{1}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\ldots}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T_{m}}})}}})={\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{Y}}
for
{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{Y}}
a fresh symbol with productions
{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{Y}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}{\mathsf{Dual}_{1}}}}}{{\longrightarrow}}{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\operatorname{word}(}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\alpha}}\ {\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T_{1}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\ldots}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T_{m}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{)}}}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{Y}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}{\mathsf{Dual}_{2}}}}}{{\longrightarrow}}{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{\varepsilon}}}}
.

Finally, let us handle the cases where
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}
is not in weak head normal form.

•

If
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}\Downarrow{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{Skip}}}}}
, then
\operatorname{word}({\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}})={\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{\varepsilon}}
.

•

Otherwise if
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}\Downarrow{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U}}\neq\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{Skip}}}
, then
\operatorname{word}({\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}})={\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{Y}}
for
{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{Y}}
a fresh nonterminal symbol. Let
{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{Z}}{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{\delta}}=\operatorname{word}({\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U}})
. Then
{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{Y}}
has a production
{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{Y}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}{a}}}}{{\longrightarrow}}{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{\gamma\delta}}
for each production
{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{Z}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}{a}}}}{{\longrightarrow}}{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{\gamma}}
.

In the above construction, we create fresh symbols each time we encounter a weak head normal form other than
\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{Skip}}}
. In other words,
\mathcal{N}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}
is the set containing
{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{\bot}}
and all nonterminals
{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{Y}}
created during the computation of
\operatorname{word}({\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}})
. Another key insight is that the sequential composition of types is translated into a concatenation of words:
\operatorname{word}({\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T_{1}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{;}}T_{2}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{;}}\ldots{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{;}}T_{n}}})=\operatorname{word}({\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T_{1}}})\operatorname{word}({\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T_{2}}})\ldots\operatorname{word}({\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T_{n}}})
. This allows our construction to terminate: even if the transitions lead to infinitely many types, they are split on the sequential composition operator, and so we only need to consider finitely many subexpressions.

For the last case in our construction to be well-defined, i.e., when
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}\Downarrow{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U}}\neq\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{Skip}}}
, we require
\operatorname{word}({\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U}})
to be non-empty. Indeed, if
{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U}}}}}\>\mathsf{whnf}
, then we can observe (by inspecting all cases) that
\operatorname{word}({\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U}})={\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{\varepsilon}}
iff
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U}}=\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{Skip}}}
.

We also need to argue that the construction of
\operatorname{word}({\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}})
eventually terminates. For this, we keep track of all types visited during the construction, and we only add a fresh nonterminal
{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{Y}}
to our grammar if the type visited is syntactically different from all types visited so far. Therefore, we reuse the same symbol
{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{Y}}
with the same productions each time we revisit a type. With all these observations, we get the following result.

Lemma 5

Suppose that
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}\in F^{\mu_{\ast};}_{\omega}
. Then the construction of
\operatorname{word}({\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}})
terminates producing a simple grammar.

We illustrate the above construction with the polymorphic tree exchanging
example from
Section

2
,

⬇

type

TreeC

a

=

&{
Leaf
:

Skip
,

Node
:

TreeC

a
;

?
a

;

TreeC

a
}

that is written in
F^{\mu_{\ast};}_{\omega}
as

{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T_{0}}}=\char 12\relax{\upsilon_{1}}{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{\textsc{t}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\mu}}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\upsilon_{2}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\colon}}{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{\textsc{s}}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{.\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\&}}\{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{Leaf}}}\colon\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{Skip}}},\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{Node}}}\colon{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{{\upsilon_{2}}};{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{?{\upsilon_{1}}}}};{{\upsilon_{2}}}}}}}}\}}}}.

Since
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T_{0}}}
is in weak head normal form,
\operatorname{word}({\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T_{0}}})
returns a
fresh symbol, which we call
{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{X_{0}}}
. We also have a production

{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{X_{0}}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}{\lambda{\upsilon_{1}}\colon{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{\kappa}}}}}}{{\longrightarrow}}{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\operatorname{word}(}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T_{1}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{)}}}}
, where

{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T_{1}}}
is the type
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\mu}}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\upsilon_{2}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\colon}}{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{\textsc{s}}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{.\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\&}}\{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{Leaf}}}\colon\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{Skip}}},\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{Node}}}\colon{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{{\upsilon_{2}}};{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{?{\upsilon_{1}}}}};{{\upsilon_{2}}}}}}}}\}}}
.
Since
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T_{1}}}
is not in whnf, we must normalise it, to get
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T_{2}}}={\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\&}}\{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{Leaf}}}\colon\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{Skip}}},\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{Node}}}\colon{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{T_{1}};{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{?{\upsilon_{1}}}}};{T_{1}}}}}}}\}}}
. Therefore
\operatorname{word}({\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T_{1}}})
returns a fresh symbol, which we call
{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{X_{1}}}
. To obtain the transitions of
{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{X_{1}}}
, we must first compute
\operatorname{word}({\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T_{2}}})
, which is a fresh symbol
{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{X_{2}}}
with transitions
{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{X_{2}}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}{\&_{1}}}}}{{\longrightarrow}}{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\operatorname{word}(}}\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{Skip}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{)}}}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{X_{2}}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}{\&_{2}}}}}{{\longrightarrow}}{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\operatorname{word}(}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{T_{1}};{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{?{\upsilon_{1}}}}};{T_{1}}}}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{)}}}}
. Thus we also get
{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{X_{1}}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}{\&_{1}}}}}{{\longrightarrow}}{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\operatorname{word}(}}\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{Skip}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{)}}}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{X_{1}}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}{\&_{2}}}}}{{\longrightarrow}}{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\operatorname{word}(}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{T_{1}};{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{?{\upsilon_{1}}}}};{T_{1}}}}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{)}}}}
.

We have
\operatorname{word}(\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{Skip}}})={\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{\varepsilon}}
, but we still need to compute

\operatorname{word}({\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{T_{1}};{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{?{\upsilon_{1}}}}};{T_{1}}}}}}})
. This type normalises to

{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T_{3}}}={\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{T_{2}};{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{?{\upsilon_{1}}}}};{T_{1}}}}}}}
since

{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T_{1}}}\Downarrow{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T_{2}}}
. Thus

\operatorname{word}({\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{T_{1}};{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{?{\upsilon_{1}}}}};{T_{1}}}}}}})
is a fresh symbol

{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{X_{3}}}
. To obtain the productions of
{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{X_{3}}}
we must compute

\operatorname{word}({\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{T_{2}};{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{?{\upsilon_{1}}}}};{T_{1}}}}}}})=\operatorname{word}({\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T_{2}}})\operatorname{word}({\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{?{\upsilon_{1}}}})\operatorname{word}({\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T_{1}}})
. At this point we
already have
\operatorname{word}({\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T_{1}}})={\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{X_{1}}}
and

\operatorname{word}({\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T_{2}}})={\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{X_{2}}}
.

We still need to compute
\operatorname{word}({\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{?{\upsilon_{1}}}})
, which is a fresh symbol
{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{X_{4}}}
with productions
{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{X_{4}}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}{?_{1}}}}}{{\longrightarrow}}{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\operatorname{word}(}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\upsilon_{1}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{)}}\bot}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{X_{4}}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}{?_{2}}}}}{{\longrightarrow}}{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{\varepsilon}}}}
. In turn,
\operatorname{word}({\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\upsilon_{1}}})
is a fresh symbol
{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{X_{5}}}
with a production
{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{X_{5}}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}{{\upsilon_{1}}}}}}{{\longrightarrow}}{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{\varepsilon}}}}
. Finally, we get
\operatorname{word}({\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{T_{2}};{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{?{\upsilon_{1}}}}};{T_{1}}}}}}})={\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{X_{2}X_{4}X_{1}}}
, which means we can write the productions for
{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{X_{3}}}
:
{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{X_{3}}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}{\&_{1}}}}}{{\longrightarrow}}{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{X_{4}X_{1}}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{X_{3}}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}{\&_{2}}}}}{{\longrightarrow}}{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{X_{3}X_{4}X_{1}}}
.

Putting all this together, we can finally obtain the simple grammar:

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{X_{0}}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}{\lambda{v_{1}}\colon{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{\textsc{t}}}}}}}{{\longrightarrow}}{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{X_{1}}}

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{X_{1}}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}{\&_{1}}}}}{{\longrightarrow}}{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{\varepsilon}}}}

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{X_{1}}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}{\&_{2}}}}}{{\longrightarrow}}{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{X_{3}}}

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{X_{2}}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}{\&_{1}}}}}{{\longrightarrow}}{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{\varepsilon}}}}

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{X_{2}}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}{\&_{2}}}}}{{\longrightarrow}}{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{X_{3}}}

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{X_{3}}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}{\&_{1}}}}}{{\longrightarrow}}{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{X_{4}X_{1}}}

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{X_{3}}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}{\&_{2}}}}}{{\longrightarrow}}{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{X_{3}X_{4}X_{1}}}

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{X_{4}}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}{?_{1}}}}}{{\longrightarrow}}{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{X_{5}\bot}}

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{X_{4}}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}{?_{2}}}}}{{\longrightarrow}}{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{\varepsilon}}}}

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{X_{5}}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}{v_{1}}}}}{{\longrightarrow}}{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{\varepsilon}}}}

Next, we argue that type equivalence (i.e., bisimilarity on types) corresponds to bisimilarity on the corresponding grammars. This is achieved by the following lemma, that asserts that the LTS of a type and the LTS of the corresponding word of nonterminals have exactly the same transitions.

Lemma 6
(Full abstraction)

Let
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}\in F^{\mu_{\ast};}_{\omega}
and
(\mathcal{T}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}},\mathcal{N}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}},\operatorname{word}({\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}),\mathcal{R}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}})
the corresponding simple grammar. Suppose also that
{{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\operatorname{word}(}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{)}}}}}\;{\approx}\;{{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{\gamma}}}
.

1.

If
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}{a}}}}{{\longrightarrow}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U}}
then there exists
{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{\gamma^{\prime}}}
such that
{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{\gamma}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}{a}}}}{{\longrightarrow}}{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{\gamma^{\prime}}}
and
{{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\operatorname{word}(}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{)}}}}}\;{\approx}\;{{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{\gamma^{\prime}}}}
.

2.

If
{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{\gamma}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}{a}}}}{{\longrightarrow}}{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{\gamma^{\prime}}}
then there exists
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U}}
such that
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}{a}}}}{{\longrightarrow}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U}}
and
{{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\operatorname{word}(}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{)}}}}}\;{\approx}\;{{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{\gamma^{\prime}}}}
.

Proof

The proof is by coinduction, showing that the relation

\mathcal{R}=\{({\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}},{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{\gamma}})\colon{{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\operatorname{word}(}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{)}}}}}\;{\approx}\;{{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{\gamma}}}\}

is backward closed for the transition relation. This is done by a case analysis on
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}
. First, assume that
{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}}}}\>\mathsf{whnf}
. We show a few cases.

•

If
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}
is
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\alpha}}}\,{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T_{1}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\ldots}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T_{m}}}}}}
, then by rules
L-Var1
and
L-Var2
the LTS at
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}
has transitions
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}{\alpha_{0}}}}}{{\longrightarrow}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{Skip}}}}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}{\alpha_{j}}}}}{{\longrightarrow}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T_{j}}}
for
1\leq j\leq m
.
Similarly,
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\operatorname{word}(}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{)}}={\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{Y}}
for
{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{Y}}
with productions
{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{Y}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}{\alpha_{0}}}}}{{\longrightarrow}}{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{\varepsilon}}}}
as well as
{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{Y}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}{\alpha_{j}}}}}{{\longrightarrow}}{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\operatorname{word}(}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T_{j}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{)}}\bot}}
for
1\leq j\leq m
.
Since
{{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{Y}}}}}\;{\approx}\;{{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{\gamma}}}
, the LTS at
{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{\gamma}}
has transitions
{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{\gamma}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}{\alpha_{0}}}}}{{\longrightarrow}}{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{\gamma_{0}}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{\gamma}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}{\alpha_{j}}}}}{{\longrightarrow}}{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{\gamma_{j}}}
for some
{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{\gamma_{0}}}
s.t.
{{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{\varepsilon}}}}}\;{\approx}\;{{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{\gamma_{0}}}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{\gamma_{j}}}
s.t.
{{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\operatorname{word}(}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T_{j}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{)}}\bot}}}\;{\approx}\;{{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{\gamma_{j}}}}
for
1\leq j\leq m
.
Since
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\operatorname{word}(}}\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{Skip}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{)}}={\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{\varepsilon}}
, we get that
(\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{Skip}}},{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{\gamma_{0}}})\in\mathcal{R}
.
Finally, since
{{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\operatorname{word}(}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T_{j}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{)}}}}}\;{\approx}\;{{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\operatorname{word}(}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T_{j}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{)}}\bot}}}
, we get that
({\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T_{j}}},{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{\gamma_{j}}})\in\mathcal{R}
for
1\leq j\leq m
.

•

If
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}
is
\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{Skip}}}
, then the LTS at
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}
has no transitions.
Similarly,
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\operatorname{word}(}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{)}}={\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{\varepsilon}}
has no transitions.
Since
{{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{\varepsilon}}}}}\;{\approx}\;{{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{\gamma}}}
, the LTS at
{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{\gamma}}
has no transitions either.

•

If
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}
is
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{U};{V}}}
, then by rule
W-Seq2

{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U}}}\>\mathsf{whnf}
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U}}
is neither
\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{Skip}}}
nor a sequential composition.
We must perform a second case analysis on
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U}}
.
For example if
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U}}
is
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\alpha}}}\,{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U_{1}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\ldots}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U_{m}}}}}}
, then by rules
L-VarSeq1
and
L-VarSeq2
the LTS at
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}
has transitions
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}{\alpha_{0}}}}}{{\longrightarrow}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{V}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}{\alpha_{j}}}}}{{\longrightarrow}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T_{j}}}
for
1\leq j\leq m
.
Similarly,
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\operatorname{word}(}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{)}}={\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\operatorname{word}(}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{)}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\operatorname{word}(}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{V}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{)}}={\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{Y}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\operatorname{word}(}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{V}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{)}}
for
{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{Y}}
with productions
{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{Y}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}{\alpha_{0}}}}}{{\longrightarrow}}{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{\varepsilon}}}}
as well as
{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{Y}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}{\alpha_{j}}}}}{{\longrightarrow}}{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\operatorname{word}(}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U_{j}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{)}}\bot}}
for
1\leq j\leq m
.
Since
{{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{Y}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\operatorname{word}(}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{V}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{)}}}}}\;{\approx}\;{{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{\gamma}}}
, the LTS at
{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{\gamma}}
has transitions
{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{\gamma}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}{\alpha_{0}}}}}{{\longrightarrow}}{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{\gamma_{0}}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{\gamma}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}{\alpha_{j}}}}}{{\longrightarrow}}{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{\gamma_{j}}}
for some
{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{\gamma_{0}}}
s.t.
{{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\operatorname{word}(}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{V}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{)}}}}}\;{\approx}\;{{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{\gamma_{0}}}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{\gamma_{j}}}
s.t.
{{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\operatorname{word}(}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U_{j}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{)}}\bot{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\operatorname{word}(}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{V}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{)}}}}}\;{\approx}\;{{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{\gamma_{j}}}}
for
1\leq j\leq m
.
We immediately get that
({\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{V}},{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{\gamma_{0}}})\in\mathcal{R}
.
Finally, since
{{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\operatorname{word}(}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U_{j}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{)}}}}}\;{\approx}\;{{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\operatorname{word}(}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U_{j}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{)}}\bot{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\operatorname{word}(}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{V}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{)}}}}}
, we get that
({\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U_{j}}},{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{\gamma_{j}}})\in\mathcal{R}
for
1\leq j\leq m
.

Next, assume that
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}
is not in whnf. There are two possibilities.

•

If
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}\Downarrow{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{Skip}}}}}
, then by rule
L-Red
the LTS at
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}
has no transitions.
Similarly,
\operatorname{word}({\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}})={\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{\varepsilon}}
has no transitions.
Since
{{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{\varepsilon}}}}}\;{\approx}\;{{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{\gamma}}}
, the LTS at
{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{\gamma}}
has no transitions either.

•

If
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}\Downarrow{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U}}\neq\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{Skip}}}
, then by rule
L-Red
the LTS at
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}
has a transition
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}{a}}}}{{\longrightarrow}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U^{\prime}}}
iff the LTS at
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U}}
has a corresponding transition
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}{a}}}}{{\longrightarrow}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U^{\prime}}}
(with the same
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U^{\prime}}}
).
Similarly,
\operatorname{word}({\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}})={\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{Y}}
for
{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{Y}}
with productions
{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{Y}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}{a}}}}{{\longrightarrow}}{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{\gamma^{\prime}\delta}}
where
{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{Z}}{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{\delta}}=\operatorname{word}({\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U}})
and
{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{Z}}
has productions
{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{Z}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}{a}}}}{{\longrightarrow}}{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{\gamma^{\prime}}}
.
Therefore, the LTS at
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\operatorname{word}(}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{)}}
has a transition
{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\operatorname{word}(}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{)}}}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}{a}}}}{{\longrightarrow}}{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{\delta^{\prime}}}
iff the LTS at
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\operatorname{word}(}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{)}}
has a transition
{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\operatorname{word}(}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{)}}}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}{a}}}}{{\longrightarrow}}{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{\delta^{\prime}}}
(with the same
{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{\delta^{\prime}}}
).
Hence
{{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\operatorname{word}(}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{)}}}}}\;{\approx}\;{{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\operatorname{word}(}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{)}}}}}{{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{}}}\;{\approx}\;{{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{\gamma}}}
, so that
({\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U}},{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{\gamma}})\in\mathcal{R}
.
Finally, since
{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U}}}\>\mathsf{whnf}
the previous case analysis shows that
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{\gamma}}
have matching transitions.
We conclude that
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{\gamma}}
have matching transitions as well.

As a consequence of the above result, we get soundness and completeness of the bisimilarity
{{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\operatorname{word}(}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{)}}}}}\;{\approx}\;{{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\operatorname{word}(}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{)}}}}}
with respect to the bisimilarity
{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}}\;{\sim}\;{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U}}}
. Indeed by
Lemma

6
, any sequence of transitions starting from
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}
can be matched by a sequence of transitions starting from
\operatorname{word}({\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}})
; and similarly for
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U}}
. Thus
{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}}\;{\sim}\;{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U}}}
iff
{{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\operatorname{word}(}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{)}}}}}\;{\approx}\;{{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\operatorname{word}(}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{)}}}}}
.

Theorem 5.1

The type equivalence problem is decidable for types in
F^{\mu_{\ast};}_{\omega}
.

Proof

Given types
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}
,
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U}}
in
F^{\mu_{\ast};}_{\omega}
, an algorithm for deciding

{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}}\;{\sim}\;{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U}}}
is as follows. First compute
\operatorname{word}({\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}})
and
\operatorname{word}({\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U}})
; this
terminates due to
Lemma

5
. Then decide whether

{{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\operatorname{word}(}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{)}}}}}\;{\approx}\;{{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\operatorname{word}(}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{)}}}}}
, using any known algorithm for
bisimilarity of simple grammars, e.g. Almeida et al.
[
6
]
or Burkart et al.
[
15
]
, whose time complexity is doubly-exponential. Correctness follows from the discussion immediately preceding this theorem.

For the remainder of this section, we look at the other classes of types in
Fig.

2
and examine the computation models they correspond to. Since class
F^{\mu;}
is contained in
F^{\mu_{\ast};}_{\omega}
, we can express types without
\lambda
-abstractions with simple grammars as well. In this way we recover previous results in the literature
[
6
,
21
]
.

Let us now look at the class
F^{\mu_{\ast}\cdot}_{\omega}
. In this class we do not have
\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{Skip}}}
nor sequential composition and message operators are binary (
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\sharp{T}}}.{U}}}
) rather than unary.
Since we do not have sequential composition, there is no need to consider words of nonterminals, and instead it suffices to translate types into single symbols, i.e., states in an automata.
Moreover, since there is no recursion beyond
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\mu}}_{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{\kappa}}}}
, only finitely many types can be reached from a given
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}
. We could thus adapt our construction as follows for
F^{\mu_{\ast}\cdot}_{\omega}
.

In the definition of the LTS (
Fig.

9
):

•

discard all rules involving sequential composition;

•

discard rules
L-Var1
for
m>0
and
L-DualVar2
(they were only needed to distinguish types in sequential composition);

•

discard case
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\iota}}=\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{End}}}
in rule
L-Const
(so that
\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{End}}}
no longer has transitions);

•

replace
\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{Skip}}}
with
\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{End}}}
on the right-hand side of rules
L-Var1
with
m=0
and
L-Const
;

•

discard rules
L-Msg1
and
L-Msg2
and treat
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\iota}}={\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\sharp}}
like the other constants in rule
L-ConstApp
.

We also replace the construction of
\operatorname{word}({\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}})
into a construction of
\operatorname{state}({\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}})
, associating to each type
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}
a state in a finite-state automata. For each transition
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}{a}}}}{{\longrightarrow}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U}}
we have the corresponding transition
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\operatorname{state}(}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{)}}}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}{a}}}}{{\longrightarrow}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\operatorname{state}(}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{)}}}}
. Notice that the resulting automata is deterministic since the original LTS is also deterministic (for each type
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}
and label
{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}{a}}
, there is at most one transition
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}{a}}}}{{\longrightarrow}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U}}
). Since bisimilarity of deterministic finite-state automata can be decided in polynomial time
[
44
]
, we get the following results.

Theorem 5.2

1.

To each type
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}
in
F^{\mu_{\ast}\cdot}_{\omega}
we can associate a finite-state automata corresponding to the (fragment of the) LTS generated by
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}
.

2.

The type equivalence problem is polynomial-time decidable for types in
F^{\mu_{\ast}\cdot}_{\omega}
.

Clearly,
Theorem

5.2
applies to the subclasses of

F^{\mu_{\ast}\cdot}_{\omega}
:
F^{\mu}
,
F^{\mu\cdot}
and
F^{\mu_{\ast}}_{\omega}
.
In this way we recover previous results in the literature

[
16
,
21
,
33
]
.

Finally, we consider the classes
F^{\mu}_{\omega}
,
F^{\mu\cdot}_{\omega}
and
F^{\mu}_{\omega}
involving arbitrarily-kinded recursion. We shall show that these classes are already powerful enough to simulate deterministic pushdown automata; hence, the type equivalence problem becomes impractical (i.e., no practical implementation of an algorithm is known). We only focus on the simplest case
F^{\mu}_{\omega}
, as the others two classes are even more expressive. Instead of looking at deterministic pushdown automata, we instead look at deterministic first-order grammars, which are an equivalent model of computation
[
46
]
. The advantage of considering deterministic first-order grammars is to simplify our construction.
We say that a
first-order grammar
is a tuple
(\mathcal{X},\mathcal{T},\mathcal{N},{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{E}},\mathcal{R})
where:

•

\mathcal{X}
is a set of variables
{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{\alpha}},{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{\beta}},\ldots
;
\mathcal{T}
is a set of terminal symbols
{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}{a}},{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}{b}},\ldots
;
\mathcal{N}
is a set of nonterminal symbols
{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{X}},{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{Y}},\ldots

•

each nonterminal
{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{X}}
has an arity
m=\operatorname{arity}({\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{X}})\in\mathbb{N}
.

•

the set
\mathcal{E}
of expressions over
\mathcal{X}
,
\mathcal{N}
is inductively defined by two rules: any variable
{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{\alpha}}
is an expression; if
\operatorname{arity}({\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{X}})=m
and
{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{E_{1}}},\ldots,{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{E_{m}}}
are expressions, then so is
{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{X\ E_{1}\ldots E_{m}}}
. Whenever
m=0
,
{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{X}}
is called a constant.

•

{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{E}}
is an expression over
\mathcal{N}
, called the initial expression.

•

\mathcal{R}
is a set of productions. Each production is a triple

({\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{X}},{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}{a}},{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{E}})
, written as

{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{X\ \alpha_{1}\ldots\alpha_{m}}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}{a}}}}{{\longrightarrow}}{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{E}}
, where
m=\operatorname{arity}({\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{X}})
and the
variables in
{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{E}}
must be taken from

{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{\alpha_{1}}},\ldots,{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{\alpha_{m}}}
.

A first-order grammar is
deterministic
if, for every
{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{X}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}{a}}
, there is at most one production
({\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{X}},{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}{a}},{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{E}})\in\mathcal{R}
.

Just as a simple grammar defines an LTS over words of nonterminals, a first-order grammar defines an LTS over the set
\mathcal{E}_{0}
of closed expressions. For each production
{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{X\ \alpha_{1}\ldots\alpha_{m}}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}{a}}}}{{\longrightarrow}}{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{E}}
we have the labelled transition
{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{X\ E_{1}\ldots E_{m}}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}{a}}}}{{\longrightarrow}}{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{E{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{[}}{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{E_{1}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{/}}{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{\alpha_{1}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{,}}\ldots{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{,}}{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{E_{m}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{/}}{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{\alpha_{m}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{]}}}}
.

Let
{{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{\!\!}}}\;{\approx}\;{{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{\!\!}}}
denote bisimilarity over closed expressions
according to a first-order grammar. We now present a fully abstract (i.e., preserving bisimilarity) translation of a deterministic first-order grammar into
a type in
F^{\mu}_{\omega}
. Each grammar variable
{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{\alpha}}
has a corresponding
type variable
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\alpha}}
(of kind
t
). An expression

{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{X\ E_{1}\ldots E_{m}}}
is represented as a type application

{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{X\ E_{1}\ldots E_{m}}}
. If
{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{X}}
has arity
m
and the productions

{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{X\ \alpha_{1}\ldots\alpha_{m}}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}{a_{j}}}}}{{\longrightarrow}}{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{E_{j}}}
for a range of
j
, then we
write the equation specifying
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{X}}
as a record (since the first-order grammar
is deterministic, all record labels are distinct, and thus the right-hand side
on the equation specifying
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{X}}
is well-formed).

{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{X}}}\;{\doteq}\;{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\char 12\relax{\alpha_{1}}{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{\textsc{t}}}{\ldots\char 12\relax{\alpha_{m}}{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{\textsc{t}}}{\{a_{1}\colon E_{1},\ldots,a_{m}\colon E_{m}\}}}}}}

This gives rise to a system of equations
\{{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{X_{i}}}}\;{\doteq}\;{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T_{i}}}}\}
, one for each nonterminal
{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{X_{i}}}
, where the nonterminals may appear in the right-hand sides
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T_{i}}}
.
Finally, given an initial expression
{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{E}}
, it is standard how to convert it into a
\mu
-type using the system above.

Using the above translation, we are able to simulate a transition
{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{E}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}{a_{j}}}}}{{\longrightarrow}}{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{F}}

of the first-order grammar as a transition
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{E}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}{\{\overline{a_{i}}\}_{j}}}}}{{\longrightarrow}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{F}}
on the corresponding types. Therefore, the translation is fully abstract and we get the following result.

Theorem 5.3

Let
{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{E}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{F}}
be closed expressions on a first-order grammar and

{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{E}},{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{F}}
the corresponding types. Then

{{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{E}}}}}\;{\approx}\;{{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{F}}}}}
iff
{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{E}}}\;{\sim}\;{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{F}}}
.

Let us work on an example to better understand the above translation. Consider the language
L_{3}=\{\ell^{n}ar^{n}a\mid n\geq 0\}\cup\{\ell^{n}br^{n}b\mid n\geq 0\}
over the alphabet
\{a,b,\ell,r\}
.
L_{3}
is a typical example of a language that cannot be described with a simple grammar, but can be accepted by a deterministic pushdown automaton
[
51
]
. Consider the first-order grammar with nonterminals
{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{X}},{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{R}},{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{A}},{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{B}},{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{\bot}}
, initial expression
{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{X}}\ {\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{A}}\ {\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{B}}
, and productions

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{X\ \alpha\ \beta}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}{\ell}}}}{{\longrightarrow}}{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{X\ (R\ \alpha)\ (R\ \beta)}}

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{X\ \alpha\ \beta}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}{a}}}}{{\longrightarrow}}{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{\alpha}}

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{X\ \alpha\ \beta}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}{b}}}}{{\longrightarrow}}{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{\beta}}

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{R\ \alpha}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}{r}}}}{{\longrightarrow}}{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{\alpha}}

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{A}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}{a}}}}{{\longrightarrow}}{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{\bot}}

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{B}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}{b}}}}{{\longrightarrow}}{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{\bot}}

Note that
{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{\bot}}
is a constant without productions. It is easy to see that the traces of this first-order grammar correspond exactly to the words in
L_{3}
. By following the steps in the above translation, we arrive at the system of equations

\displaystyle{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{X}}}\;{\doteq}\;{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\char 12\relax\alpha{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{\textsc{t}}}{\char 12\relax\beta{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{\textsc{t}}}{\{\ell\colon X(R\alpha)(R\beta),a\colon\alpha,b\colon\beta\}}}}}}

\displaystyle{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{R}}}\;{\doteq}\;{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\char 12\relax\alpha{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{\textsc{t}}}{\{r\colon\alpha\}}}}}

\displaystyle{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{A}}}\;{\doteq}\;{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\{a\colon\bot\}}}}

\displaystyle{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{B}}}\;{\doteq}\;{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\{b\colon\bot\}}}}

\displaystyle{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\bot}}}\;{\doteq}\;{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\{\}}}}

Therefore, the initial expression
{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{X\ A\ B}}
becomes the type

{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{({\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\mu}}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\xi}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\colon}}{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{\textsc{t}}}}\Rightarrow{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{\textsc{t}}}}\Rightarrow{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{\textsc{t}}}}}}}}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{.\,\char 12\relax\alpha{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{\textsc{t}}}{\char 12\relax\beta{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{\textsc{t}}}{\{\ell\colon\xi\{r\colon\alpha\}\{r\colon\beta\},a\colon\alpha,b\colon\beta\}}})\{a\colon\{\}\}\{b\colon\{\}\}}}}},

whose transitions simulate the transitions of the first-order grammar.

6
The term language and its metatheory

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{v}}\;::=\;\

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{c}}\;\mid\;{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{x}}\;\mid\;{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{\lambda{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{x}}\colon{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}.{t}}}\;\mid\;{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{rec}}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{x}}\colon{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}.{v}}}\;\mid\;{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{\Lambda{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\alpha}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\colon}}{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{\kappa}}}}.{v}}}\;\mid\;{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{\{\overline{{l_{i}}={v_{i}}}\}}}\;\mid\;{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{\langle{l}={v}\rangle\,\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{as}}}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}}}

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{receive}}}}[{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}]}}\;\mid\;{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{{{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{receive}}}}[{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}]}}}[{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}]}}\;\mid\;{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{send}}}}[{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}]}}\;\mid\;{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{send}}}}[{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}]}}}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{v}}\;\mid\;{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{{{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{send}}}}[{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}]}}}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{v}}}[{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}]}}

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{t}}\;::=\;\

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{v}}\;\mid\;{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{t}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{t}}\;\mid\;{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{{t}[{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}]}}\;\mid\;{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{\{\overline{{l_{i}}={t_{i}}}\}}}\;\mid\;{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{let}}}\,\{\overline{{l_{i}}={x_{i}}}\}={t}\,\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{in}}}\,{t}}}

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{\langle{l}={t}\rangle\,\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{as}}}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}}}\;\mid\;{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{case}}}\,{t}\,\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{of}}}\,{t}}}\;\mid\;{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{match}}}\,{t}\,\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{with}}}\,{t}}}

\displaystyle\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{new}}}

channel creation

Figure 11
:
Terms, and typed for term constants.

This section briefly introduces a concurrent functional language equipped with

F^{\mu_{\ast};}_{\omega}
types, together with its metatheory. The results mostly follow from those in the literature, although explicit recursion at the term level and the unrestricted bindings in typing contexts are somewhat new in session types.

The syntax of values, terms, processes and call-by-value evaluation contexts are
defined by the grammar in
Fig.

11
. The same figure introduces
types for the constants.
The term language is essentially the polymorphic lambda calculus with support
for session operators, formulated as in Almeida et al. and Cai et al.
[
2
,
16
]
.
From System
F
it comprises terms and type abstractions, records and variants,
including constructors and destructors in each case.
The support for session operations and concurrency includes channel creation
(
\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{new}}}
), the different channel operations (
\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{receive}}}
,
\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{send}}}
,
\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{match}}}
,

\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{select}}}
and
\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{close}}}
) and thread creation (
\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{fork}}}
).
We program at the term level and use processes only for the runtime. Processes
include terms as threads, parallel composition and channel creation, all
inspired in the pi-calculus with double
binders
[
73
]
.

Term typing
{\Delta}\mid{\Gamma}\vdash{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{t}}\colon{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}

\frac{{\Delta}\vdash{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T_{c}}}}\;{:}\;{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{*}}}}}}{{\Delta}\mid{\cdot}\vdash{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{c}}\colon{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T_{c}}}}\quad\textsc{T-{Const} }\par\frac{}{{\Delta}\mid{{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{x}}\colon{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}}\vdash{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{x}}\colon{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}}\quad\textsc{T-{Var} }\par\frac{{\Delta}\mid{\Gamma_{1}}\vdash{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{t_{1}}}\colon{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U\rightarrow T}}\quad{\Delta}\mid{\Gamma_{2}}\vdash{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{t_{2}}}\colon{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U}}}{{\Delta}\mid{\Gamma_{1},\Gamma_{2}}\vdash{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{t_{1}}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{t_{2}}}}}\colon{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}}\quad\textsc{T-{TApp} }\par\frac{{\Delta}\vdash{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}}\;{:}\;{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{*}}}}}\quad{\Delta}\mid{\Gamma,{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{x}}\colon^{\!\!\!\omega}\>{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T\rightarrow U}}.{v}}}}}\colon{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T\rightarrow U}}}\quad\textsc{T-{Rec} }\par\frac{\overline{{\Delta}\mid{\Gamma_{i}}\vdash{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{t_{i}}}\colon{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T_{i}}}}}{{\Delta}\mid{\overline{\Gamma}}\vdash{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{\{\overline{{l_{i}}={t_{i}}}\}}}}}\colon{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\{\overline{{l_{i}}\colon{T_{i}}}\}}}}}}\quad\textsc{T-{Record} }\par\frac{{\Delta}\mid{\Gamma_{1}}\vdash{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{t_{1}}}\colon{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\{\overline{{l_{i}}\colon{T_{i}}}\}}}}}\quad{\Delta}\mid{\Gamma_{2},\overline{{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{x_{i}}}\colon{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T_{i}}}}}\vdash{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{t_{2}}}\colon{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}}{{\Delta}\mid{\Gamma_{1},\Gamma_{2}}\vdash{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{let}}}\,\{\overline{{l_{i}}={x_{i}}}\}={t_{1}}\,\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{in}}}\,{t_{2}}}}}}\colon{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}}\quad\textsc{T-{Proj} }\par\frac{{\Delta}\mid{\Gamma_{1}}\vdash{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{t_{1}}}\colon{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\&\{\overline{l_{i}:{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}_{i}}\}}}}}\quad{\Delta}\mid{\Gamma_{2}}\vdash{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{t_{2}}}\colon{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\{\overline{{l_{i}}\colon{T_{i}\rightarrow T}}\}}}}}}{{\Delta}\mid{\Gamma_{1},\Gamma_{2}}\vdash{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{match}}}\,{t_{1}}\,\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{with}}}\,{t_{2}}}}}}\colon{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}}\quad\textsc{T-{Match} }\quad\frac{{\Delta}\mid{\Gamma}\vdash{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{t}}\colon{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U}}\quad{\Delta}\vdash{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U}}}\;{:}\;{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{*}}}}}\quad{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U}}}\;{\sim}\;{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}}}{{\Delta}\mid{\Gamma}\vdash{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{t}}\colon{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}}\quad\textsc{T-{Eq}
