}\par\frac{{\Delta}\mid{\Gamma,{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{y}}\colon^{\!\!\!\omega}\>{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}},{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{z}}\colon^{\!\!\!\omega}\>{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}}\vdash{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{t}}\colon{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U}}}{{\Delta}\mid{\Gamma,{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{x}}\colon^{\!\!\!\omega}\>{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}}\vdash{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{t{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{[}}{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{x}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{/}}{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{y}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{]}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{[}}{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{x}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{/}}{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{z}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{]}}}}\colon{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U}}}\quad\textsc{T-{Contraction} }

Process typing
{\Gamma}\vdash{{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{p}}}

\frac{{\varepsilon}\mid{\Gamma}\vdash{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{t}}\colon{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{Unit}}}}}}{{\Gamma}\vdash{{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{\langle{t}\rangle}}}}}}\par\frac{{\Gamma_{1}}\vdash{{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{p_{1}}}}\quad{\Gamma_{2}}\vdash{{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{p_{2}}}}}{{\Gamma_{1},\Gamma_{2}}\vdash{{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{p_{1}\mid p_{2}}}}}\par\frac{{\Gamma,{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{x}}\colon{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}},{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{y}}\colon{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{Dual}}}}\,{T}}}}}}\vdash{{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{p}}}}{{\Gamma}\vdash{{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{(\nu{x}{y}){p}}}}}}}

(T-App, T-Abs: see Cai et al.
[
16
]
; T-Case adapt from T-Match)

Figure 12
:
Typing.

Term and process typing are in
Fig.

12
.
A judgement of the form
{\Delta}\mid{\Gamma}\vdash{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{t}}\colon{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}
records the fact that term

{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{t}}
has type
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}
under contexts
\Delta
(recording the kinds of
type variables) and
\Gamma
(recording types for term variables).
The judgement for processes,
{\Gamma}\vdash{{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{p}}}
, says that
{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{p}}
is
well-typed under context
\Gamma
. The judgement simplifies that for terms, for
processes feature no free type variables and are assigned no particular type.
Here
\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{Unit}}}
is short for the empty record type

{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\{\}}}
, and
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T\otimes U}}
is short for the record type

{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{Fst}}}\colon T,\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{Snd}}}\colon U\}}}
.
Once again, the rules are adapted from the two above cited works. The difference
to Cai et al.
[
16
]
is that we work with in a linear
setting and hence axioms (T-Const and T-Var) work on an empty context, and
most of the other rules must split the context accordingly. Rule T-TAbs simplifies that of Cai et al.
[
16
]
; we can easily show
that the two rules are interchangeable.
We support exponentials
[
37
]
for recursive
functions, so that one may write functions that feature more than one recursive
call (good for consuming binary trees, for example) and branches that do not use
the recursive function (for code that is supposed to terminate). Towards this
end, we add an unrestricted binding
{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{x}}\colon^{\!\!\!\omega}\>{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}
in term variable contexts, an
explicit rule for
\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{rec}}}
(as opposed to making
\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{rec}}}
a constant as in Cai et al.
[
16
]
) and structural rules for unrestricted bindings
(T-Dereliction, T-Weakening and T-Contraction).
Thanks to the power of System
F
, most of the session and concurrency operators
are expressed as constants. For example,
\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{receive}}}
receives a session type

{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{!{\alpha}}}.{\beta}}}
with
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\alpha}}
, the payload of the message, an arbitrary type
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\beta}}
, the continuation, a session type, and returns a pair of the value
received and the continuation channel. As usual
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\forall{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\alpha}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\colon}}{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{\kappa}}}}.\,T}}
abbreviates the type
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\forall}}_{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{\kappa}}}}}\,{(\char 12\relax\alpha{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{\kappa}}T)}}}
. The
exception is the external choice (T-Match) which can not be captured by a type
(similarly to T-Case) and hence requires a dedicated typing rule.

Term reduction
{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{t}}\rightarrow{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{t}}

\frac{}{{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{({\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{\lambda{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{x}}\colon{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}.{t}}})}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{v}}}}\rightarrow{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{t}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{[}}{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{v}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{/}}{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{x}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{]}}}}}\quad\frac{}{{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{({\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{{{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{\Lambda{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\alpha}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\colon}}{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{\kappa}}}}.{t}}})}[{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}]}}}}\rightarrow{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{t}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{[}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{/}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\alpha}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{]}}}}}\quad\frac{}{{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{let}}}\,\{\overline{{l_{i}}={x_{i}}}\}={{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{\{\overline{{l_{i}}={v_{i}}}\}}}}\,\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{in}}}\,{t}}}}}\rightarrow{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{t}}\overline{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{[}}{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{v_{i}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{/}}{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{x_{i}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{]}}}}}}\par\frac{}{{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{case}}}\,{({\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{\langle{l_{j}}={v}\rangle\,\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{as}}}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}}})}\,\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{of}}}\,{{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{\{\overline{{l_{i}}={t_{i}}}\}}}}}}}}\rightarrow{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{t_{j}}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{v}}}}}\par\par\frac{}{{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{({\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{rec}}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{x}}\colon{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}.{v}}})}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{u}}}}\rightarrow{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{(v{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{[}}{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{rec}}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{x}}\colon{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}.{v}}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{/}}{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{x}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{]}})}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{u}}}}}\par\frac{{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{t_{1}}}\rightarrow{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{t_{2}}}}{{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{E[t_{1}]}}\rightarrow{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{E[t_{2}]}}}

Structural congruence (congruence rules omitted)
{{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{p}}}\;{\equiv}\;{{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{p}}}

\displaystyle{{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{p_{1}\mid p_{2}}}}\;{\equiv}\;{{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{p_{2}\mid p_{1}}}}\qquad{{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{(p_{1}\mid p_{2})\mid p_{3}}}}\;{\equiv}\;{{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{p_{1}\mid(p_{2}\mid p_{3})}}}

\displaystyle{{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{(\nu{x}{y}){p_{1}}}}\mid p_{2}}}}\;{\equiv}\;{{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{(\nu{x}{y}){(p_{1}\mid p_{2})}}}}}}\qquad{{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{(\nu{w}{x}){{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{(\nu{y}{z}){p}}}}}}}}}\;{\equiv}\;{{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{(\nu{y}{z}){{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{(\nu{w}{x}){p}}}}}}}}}

Process reduction
{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{p}}\rightarrow{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{p}}

\frac{{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{t_{1}}}\rightarrow{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{t_{2}}}}{{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{\langle{t_{1}}\rangle}}}}\rightarrow{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{\langle{t_{2}}\rangle}}}}}\par\frac{}{{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{\langle{E[{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{fork}}}}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{v}}]}\rangle}}}}\rightarrow{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{\langle{E[{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{\{\}}}]}\rangle}}\mid{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{\langle{{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{v}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{\{\}}}}}}\rangle}}}}}\par\frac{}{{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{\langle{E[{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{new}}}}[{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}]}}]}\rangle}}}}\rightarrow{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{(\nu{x}{y}){{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{\langle{E[{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{\{{x},{y}\}}}]}\rangle}}}}}}}}\par\frac{}{{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{(\nu{x}{y}){({\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{\langle{E_{1}[{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{{{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{receive}}}}[{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}]}}}[{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U}}]}}}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{y}}}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{]}}}\rangle}}\mid{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{\langle{E_{2}[{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{{{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{send}}}}[{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{V}}]}}}[{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{W}}]}}}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{v}}}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{x}}]}\rangle}})}}}}}\rightarrow{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{(\nu{x}{y}){({\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{\langle{E_{1}[{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{\{{y},{v}\}}}]}\rangle}}\mid{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{\langle{E_{2}[x]}\rangle}})}}}}}}\par\frac{}{{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{(\nu{x}{y}){({\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{\langle{E_{1}[{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{match}}}\,{y}\,\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{with}}}\,{{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{\{\overline{{l_{i}}={t_{i}}}\}}}}}}]}\rangle}}\mid{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{\langle{E_{2}[{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{(\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{select}}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{l_{j}}}\,\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{as}}}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}})}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{x}}]}\rangle}})}}}}}\rightarrow{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{(\nu{x}{y}){{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{\langle{E_{1}[{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{t_{j}}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{y}}]}\rangle}}\mid{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{\langle{E_{2}[x]}\rangle}}}}}}}}\par\frac{}{{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{(\nu{x}{y}){({\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{\langle{E_{1}[{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{close}}}}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{y}}]}\rangle}}\mid{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{\langle{E_{2}[{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{close}}}}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{x}}]}\rangle}})}}}}}\rightarrow{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{\langle{E_{1}[{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{\{\}}}]}\rangle}}\mid{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{\langle{E_{2}[{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{\{\}}}]}\rangle}}}}}\par\frac{{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{p_{1}}}\rightarrow{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{p_{2}}}}{{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{p_{1}\mid q}}\rightarrow{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{p_{2}\mid q}}}\par\frac{{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{p_{1}}}\rightarrow{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{p_{2}}}}{{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{(\nu{x}{y}){p_{1}}}}}}\rightarrow{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{(\nu{x}{y}){p_{2}}}}}}}\par\frac{{{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{p_{1}}}}\;{\equiv}\;{{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{p_{2}}}}\quad{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{p_{2}}}\rightarrow{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{p_{3}}}\quad{{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{p_{3}}}}\;{\equiv}\;{{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{p_{4}}}}}{{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{p_{1}}}\rightarrow{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{p_{4}}}}

Figure 13
:
Term and process reduction.

Term and process reduction are in
Fig.

13
. Term reduction
comprises the standard axioms in System F with records, variants and recursion.
Evaluation contexts greatly simplify the structural reduction rules and pave the
way to process reduction. Following Milner
[
55
]
we
factor out processes by means of a structural congruence relation that accounts
for the associative and commutative nature of parallel composition, scope
extrusion and exchanging the order of channel bindings. The rules closely follow
Almeida et al. and Gay and
Vasconcelos
[
2
,
35
]
.
One finds axioms for forking new threads, creating new channels, for
communication (
\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{receive}}}
/
\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{send}}}
,
\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{match}}}
/
\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{select}}}
and

\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{close}}}
/
\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{close}}}
), as well as structural rules to allow reduction underneath
parallel composition, channel creation and structural congruence.

We now address the metatheory of our language, starting with preservation for
both terms and processes.

Theorem 6.1
(Preservation)

1.

If
{\Delta}\mid{\Gamma}\vdash{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{t}}\colon{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{t}}\rightarrow{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{t^{\prime}}}
, then

{\Delta}\mid{\Gamma}\vdash{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{t^{\prime}}}\colon{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}
.

2.

If
{\Gamma}\vdash{{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{p}}}
and
{{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{p}}}\;{\equiv}\;{{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{p^{\prime}}}}
, then
{\Gamma}\vdash{{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{p^{\prime}}}}
.

3.

If
{\Gamma}\vdash{{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{p}}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{p}}\rightarrow{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{p^{\prime}}}
, then
{\Gamma}\vdash{{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{p^{\prime}}}}
.

Progress for the term language is assured only when the typing context contains
channel endpoints only. When
\Delta
is understood from the context we write

\Gamma^{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{\textsc{s}}}}
to mean that
\Gamma
contains only types of kind
s
,
that is
{\Delta}\vdash{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}}\;{:}\;{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{\textsc{s}}}}}}
for all types
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}
in
\Gamma
. Well
typed terms are values, or else they may reduce or are ready to reduce at the
process level. Reduction in the case of session operations—
\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{receive}}}
,

\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{send}}}
,
\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{match}}}
,
\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{select}}}
,
\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{close}}}
—is pending a matching counterpart.

Theorem 6.2
(Progress for the term language)

If
{\Delta}\mid{\Gamma^{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{\textsc{s}}}}}\vdash{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{t}}\colon{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}
, then
{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{t}}
is a value,

{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{t}}
reduces, or
{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{t}}
is stuck in one of the following
forms:
{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{E[{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{fork}}}}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{v}}]}}
,

{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{E[{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{new}}}}[{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}]}}]}}
,

{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{E[{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{{{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{receive}}}}[{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}]}}}[{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U}}]}}}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{v}}}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{]}}}}
,

{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{E[{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{{{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{send}}}}[{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U}}]}}}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{T}}}[{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{v}}]}}}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{x}}]}}
,

{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{E[{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{match}}}\,{y}\,\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{with}}}\,{{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{\{\overline{{l_{i}}={t_{i}}}\}}}}}}]}}
,

{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{E[{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{(\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{select}}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{l_{j}}}\,\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{as}}}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}})}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{x}}]}}
, or

{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{E[{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{close}}}}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{x}}]}}
.

In order to state our result on the absence of runtime errors we need a few
notions on the structure of terms and processes; here we follow
Almeida et al.
[
2
]
.
The
subject
of an expression
{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{e}}
, denoted by
\operatorname{subj}({\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{e}})
, is
{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{x}}
in the following cases.

{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{{{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{receive}}}}[{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}]}}}[{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U}}]}}}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{x}}\quad{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{{{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{send}}}}[{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}]}}}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{v}}}[{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U}}]}}}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{x}}\quad{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{match}}}\,{x}\,\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{with}}}\,{t}}}\qquad{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{(\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{select}}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{l_{j}}}\,\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{as}}}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}})}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{x}}\qquad{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{close}}}}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{x}}

Two terms
{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{e_{1}}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{e_{2}}}

agree
on channel
{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{xy}}
,
notation
\operatorname{agree}^{{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{xy}}}({\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{e_{1}}},{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{e_{2}}})
, in the following cases (symmetric forms
omitted).

\displaystyle\operatorname{agree}^{{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{xy}}}({\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{{{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{receive}}}}[{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}]}}}[{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U}}]}}}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{x}}}},{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{{{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{send}}}}[{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{V}}]}}}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{v}}}[{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{W}}]}}}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{y}}}})\qquad\operatorname{agree}^{{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{xy}}}({\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{close}}}}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{x}}}},{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{close}}}}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{y}}}})

\displaystyle\operatorname{agree}^{{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{xy}}}({\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{match}}}\,{x}\,\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{with}}}\,{{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{\{\overline{{l_{i}}={t_{i}}}\}}}_{i\in I}}}}}},{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{(\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{select}}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{l_{j}}}\,\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{as}}}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}})}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{y}}}})\quad j\in I

A closed process is a
runtime error
if it is structural congruent to
some process that contains a subexpression or subprocess of one of the
following forms.

1.

{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{v}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{u}}
where
{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{v}}
is not a
{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{\lambda}}
or a
\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{rec}}}
,

{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{v}}\neq{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{{{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{receive}}}}[{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}]}}}[{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U}}]}}
,

{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{send}}}}[{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}]}}}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{u}}
,

{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{{{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{send}}}}[{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}]}}}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{w}}}[{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U}}]}}
,

\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{select}}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{l_{j}}}\,\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{as}}}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}
,

\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{close}}}
,

\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{fork}}}
;

2.

{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{{v}[{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}]}}
where
{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{v}}
in not a
{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{\Lambda}}
,

{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{v}}\neq\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{receive}}}
,

{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{receive}}}}[{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U}}]}}
,

\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{send}}}
,

{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{send}}}}[{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U}}]}}
,

\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{new}}}
;

3.

{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{let}}}\,\{\overline{{l_{i}}={x_{i}}}\}={v}\,\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{in}}}\,{t}}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{v}}
is not of the form

{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{\{\overline{{l_{i}}={u_{i}}}\}}}
;

4.

{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{case}}}\,{v}\,\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{of}}}\,{t}}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{v}}\neq{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{\langle{l_{j}}={u}\rangle\,\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{as}}}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}}}
or

{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{t}}\neq{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{\{\overline{{l_{i}}={u_{i}}}\}}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{i\in I}}}
with
j\notin I
;

5.

{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{{{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{receive}}}}[{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}]}}}[{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U}}]}}}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{v}}
or

{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{{{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{send}}}}[{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}]}}}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{u}}}[{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U}}]}}}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{v}}
or

{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{match}}}\,{v}\,\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{with}}}\,{t}}}
or

{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{(\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{select}}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{l}}\,\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{as}}}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}})}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{v}}
or

{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{close}}}}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{v}}
and

{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{v}}
is not an endpoint
{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{x}}
;

6.

{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{\langle{E_{1}[e_{1}]}\rangle}}\mid{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{\langle{E_{2}[e_{2}]}\rangle}}
and
\operatorname{subj}({\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{e_{1}}})=\operatorname{subj}({\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{e_{2}}})
;

7.

{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{(\nu{x}{y}){({\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{\langle{E_{1}[e_{1}]}\rangle}}\mid{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{\langle{E_{2}[e_{2}]}\rangle}})}}}
and

\operatorname{subj}({\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{e_{1}}})={\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{x}}
and
\operatorname{subj}({\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{e_{2}}})={\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{y}}
and

\neg\operatorname{agree}^{{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{xy}}}({\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{e_{1}}},{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{e_{2}}})
.

The first five cases are standard to system
F
with records and variants. The
support for session types and concurrency in the first two cases (term and type
application) are derived from the types of values for such operators
(
Fig.

11
).
Item

5
addresses session operators
applied to non endpoints.
Item

6
is for two concurrent
session operators on the same channel end. Finally,
Item

7
is
for mismatches on two session operations on two endpoints for the same channel.

Theorem 6.3
(Safety)

If
{\Gamma^{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{\textsc{s}}}}}\vdash{{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{p}}}
, then
{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{p}}
is not a runtime error.

An
algorithmic typing system
can be easily extracted from the declarative
system for terms in
Fig.

12
via a bidirectional type system. The
system, formulated along the lines of Almeida et al.
[
2
]
,
converts the non-syntax directed judgement
{\Delta}\mid{\Gamma}\vdash{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{t}}\colon{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}
into two
functional judgements: type synthesis

{\Delta}\mid{\Gamma_{1}}\vdash{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{t}}\Rightarrow{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}\mid{\Gamma_{2}}
and type check

{\Delta}\mid{\Gamma_{1}}\vdash{\color[rgb]{0.59,0,0.66}{t}}\Leftarrow{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}\mid{\Gamma_{2}}
. In the algorithmic type system,
context
\Gamma_{2}
contains the unused part of context
\Gamma_{1}

[
73
,
74
,
75
]
.

7
Related Work

We briefly discuss work that is closer to ours.

Equirecursion in system
F
.

In first investigations on equirecursive types, the notion of type equivalence
is often formulated in a coinductive
fashion
[
7
,
13
,
20
,
29
,
38
]
.
Two types are equivalent if they unroll into the same infinite tree. Whenever
this unrolling is the only type-level computation, such trees are regular,
enabling efficient decision procedures. Some authors have studied equirecursion
together with other notions of type-level computation. Solomon considers parameterized
type definitions, which correspond to higher-order
kinds
[
63
]
. These implicitly correspond to
\lambda
-terms,
since reduction occurs as types are allowed to call other types. Some
authors consider equirecursion in system
F_{\omega}
, with weaker or stronger
notions of
equality
[
1
,
14
,
16
,
41
]
.
Regarding equirecursion in system
F
, the model of
Cai et al.
[
16
]
is the closest to ours, and indeed our results up
to
F^{\mu_{\ast}\cdot}_{\omega}
can be seen as a generalization of theirs.
However, Cai et al. depart from the usual setting by
allowing non-contractive types (which most authors forbid, including this work),
requiring a sort of infinitary lambda calculus. Moreover, this work further
extends additional equivalence properties by including session types with their
distinctive semantics, such as sequential composition and duality.

Session type systems.

Session types were introduced in the 90s by Honda et al.
[
42
,
43
,
67
]
.
Equirecursion was the first approach used to construct infinite session types,
which often allows type equality to be interpreted according to a coinductive
notion of bisimulation
[
52
]
. In this vein, Keizer et al.
[
48
]
utilize coalgebras to represent session
types. Since the inception of session types,
there has been an interest in extending the theory to nonregular
protocols
[
58
,
59
,
66
]
.
Context-free session types emerged as a natural extension, as it still allowed
for practical type equality
algorithms
[
4
,
5
,
6
,
21
,
56
,
68
]
.
Other approaches that go beyond regular session types include nested session
types
[
25
]
as well as 1-counter, pushdown and
2-counter session types
[
33
]
. However, the
more expressive notions are not amenable to practical type equivalence
algorithms, just like the higher-order types present in our system

F^{\mu}_{\omega}
. Polymorphism in session types has also been a topic of interest,
with or without
recursion
[
17
,
23
,
24
,
31
,
39
]
.

Dual type operator.

This work is, to the best of our knowledge, the first that internalises duality as a type constructor. Other settings, such as the language Alms
[
72
]
, consider duality for session types as a user-definable, not built in, type function. Our
\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{Dual}}}
is a type operator, not a type function. The difference is that a type function involves a type-level computation, which converges to a type written without dual. For example, in Alms we would have
\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{dual}}}({\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{!{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{Int}}}}}}.{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{End}}}}}})={\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{?{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{Int}}}}}}.{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{End}}}}}}
(as a type-level computation), both sides being the
same
type. In our setting,
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{Dual}}}}\,{({\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{!{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{Int}}}}}}};{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{End}}}}}})}}}
is a type on its own, which happens to be equivalent to
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{?{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{Int}}}}}}};{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{End}}}}}}
. At the same time, our setting allows for types such as
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{Dual}}}}\,{\alpha}}}
, or
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{({\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{Dual}}}}\,{\alpha}}})};{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{T_{1}};{T_{2}}}}}}}
, which do not reduce.

Type equivalence algorithms.

Algorithms for deciding the equivalence of types must inherently be related to
the computational power of the corresponding type system. This has been used
implicitly or explicitly to obtain decidability results. As already explained,
if equirecursion is the only type-level computation, types can be represented as
finite-state automata (or equivalently, infinite regular trees). Although some
exponential time algorithms were first
proposed
[
32
]
, it has been established that the
problem can be solved in quadratic time
[
53
]
, which
is to be expected as it matches the corresponding problem of bisimulation of
finite-state automata
[
44
]
; see also Pierce
[
57
]
.

The next ‘simplest’ model of computation is that of simple grammars, which
intuitively correspond to deterministic pushdown automata with a single
state
[
33
]
.
Almeida et al.
[
6
]
provided a practical algorithm for checking the bisimilarity of simple grammars. By dropping the determinism assumption, we arrive at Greibach normal form grammars, which are equivalent to basic process algebras
[
8
,
9
]
. Bisimilarity algorithms have been studied extensively in this setting
[
15
,
19
,
47
,
49
]
; presently it is known that the complexity of the problem lies between EXPTIME and 2-EXPTIME, which does not exclude the possibility of a polynomial time algorithm for the simpler model of simple grammars.

In this paper we present a reduction from first-order grammars to
F^{\mu}_{\omega}
-types, showing that the more expressive type systems (
F^{\mu}_{\omega}
, presented here and in Cai et al.
[
16
]
, as well as its extensions) are at least as powerful as deterministic pushdown automata.
As far as we know, the closest result to ours is by Solomon
[
63
]
, which shows conversions between a universe of “context-free types” and deterministic context-free languages. The universe of types studied by Solomon is different from
F^{\mu}_{\omega}
. With some work we could prove that Solomon’s types can be embedded into
F^{\mu}_{\omega}
, which would entail our result as a corollary. However, it is easier and simpler to prove directly the reduction as we did.

The equivalence problem for deterministic pushdown automata was a notorious open problem for a long time, until Sénizergues showed it to be decidable
[
61
,
62
]
. Since his proof, many authors have tried to refine the result in an attempt to arrive at an implementable algorithm
[
46
,
64
,
65
]
.

Concurrent term languages.

The usefulness of a type system is directly related to its capability to be used
in a programming language. Type systems such as the ones discussed in this work
lend themselves quite readily to functional term
languages
[
45
]
. For session types, existing term
languages are either inspired in the pi
calculus
[
27
,
73
,
69
]

or in the lambda
calculus
[
35
,
54
,
70
]
,
or even the two
[
71
]
. The system presented in
this paper is linear, meaning that resources must be used exactly
once
[
50
,
74
]
.
Some authors go beyond linearity by considering unrestricted type
qualifiers
[
48
,
73
]
or
manifest sharing
[
10
]
.

8
Conclusion and future work

This paper introduces an extension of system
F
which includes equirecursion,
lambda abstractions, and context-free session types. We present type equivalence
algorithms, and a term language and its metatheory. Although we have defined a
rather general system, it turns out that for practical purposes one must
restrict recursion to
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\mu}}_{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{*}}}}
, that is, to type-level monomorphic recursion.
In any case, the main system
F^{\mu_{\ast};}_{\omega}
is a non-trivial extension of
(the contractive fragment of)
F^{\mu_{\ast}}_{\omega}
(studied by Cai
et al.
[
16
]
) as well as
F^{\mu;}
(studied by Almeida
et al.
[
21
]
).

We have only considered polymorphic types of a functional nature: type

{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\forall{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\alpha}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\colon}}{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{\kappa}}}}.\,T}}
must always be of kind
t
. It is worth
investigating polymorphism over session types, as it would allow further
additional behaviour. For example, we could be interested in streaming values of
heterogeneous nature, as in type

{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\mu}}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\alpha}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\colon}}{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{\textsc{s}}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{.\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\&}}\{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{Done}}}\colon\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{Skip}}},\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{More}}}\colon{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\forall{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\beta}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\colon}}{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{\textsc{t}}}}}.\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{?{\beta}}}}}};{\alpha}}}\}}}
.
It is however unclear whether this extension would still allow a translation into a simple grammar.

We proved that the type equivalence problem for systems
F^{\mu}_{\omega}
,
F^{\mu\cdot}_{\omega}
,
F^{\mu;}_{\omega}
is at least as hard as a non-efficiently-decidable problem. We conjecture that these systems have the same power as deterministic pushdown automata (and hence, admit decidable type equivalence), but we do not have a construction to prove this result. In any case, our proof that the type equivalence problem is at least as hard as the bisimilarity of deterministic pushdown automata is enough to justify focus on the significant fragment with restricted recursion.

We study either full recursion (for theoretical results) or recursion limited to
kind
{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{*}}
(for algorithmic results). It would be interesting to study
in-between kinds of recursion; the next natural example is

{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\mu}}_{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{*}}}\Rightarrow{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{*}}}}}}}
. What model of computation would we arrive at if we
consider types written with this recursion operator? We conjecture that types

F^{\mu}_{\omega}
and
F^{\mu\cdot}_{\omega}
, when restricted to recursion of kind

{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{*}}}\Rightarrow{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{*}}}}}
, would still be expressible as simple grammars, whereas such
a restriction in the more powerful
F^{\mu;}_{\omega}
would take us beyond this
model, but perhaps without reaching the expressivity of deterministic pushdown
automata.

Appendix 0.A
Proof of
Theorem

3.2

This section is devoted to proving
Theorem

3.2
, that kinding is decidable. Since kinding requires normalisation (due to rule
K-TApp
), we must first investigate that problem.

Even without considering sequential composition and recursion, it is well-known that normalisation may not terminate (
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{(\lambda x.x\ x)\ (\lambda x.x\ x)}}
is the typical example); in fact it is undecidable whether a type normalises.
The standard approach is to consider kinded types, which are strongly normalising.
However, in our model kinding itself requires normalisation, which leads to a “chicken-and-egg” situation. The solution is to consider a notion of
pre-kinding
. We will write
{\Delta}\vdash_{\mathsf{pre}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}}\;{:}\;{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{\kappa}}}}}
to mean that
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}
is pre-kinded with kind
{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{\kappa}}
. The rules for pre-kinding are the same as for kinding, with the exception of rule
K-TApp
which loses the normalisation proviso, becoming rule
PK-TApp
:

\frac{{\Delta}\vdash{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}}\;{:}\;{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{\kappa}}}\Rightarrow{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{\kappa}}^{\prime}}}}}}}\quad\;{\Delta}\vdash{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U}}}\;{:}\;{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{\kappa}}}}}\quad\;{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{T}\,{U}}}}}}\>\mathsf{norm}}{{\Delta}\vdash{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{T}\,{U}}}}}}\;{:}\;{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{\kappa}}^{\prime}}}}}\quad\textsc{\text{\small\sc K-{TApp}} }\Rightarrow\frac{{\Delta}\vdash_{\mathsf{pre}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}}\;{:}\;{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{\kappa}}}\Rightarrow{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{\kappa}}^{\prime}}}}}}}\quad\;{\Delta}\vdash_{\mathsf{pre}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U}}}\;{:}\;{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{\kappa}}}}}}{{\Delta}\vdash_{\mathsf{pre}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{T}\,{U}}}}}}\;{:}\;{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{\kappa}}^{\prime}}}}}\quad\textsc{\text{\small\sc PK-TApp} }

Lemma 7

{\Delta}\vdash_{\mathsf{pre}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}}\;{:}\;{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{\kappa}}}}}
is decidable (in linear time).

Proof

Pre-kinding uses rule
PK-TApp
instead of
PK-TApp
, and therefore does not require normalisation. Therefore, given
\Delta
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}
a pre-kind can be inferred by traversing the abstract syntax tree defining
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}
, and using the context
\Delta
to infer the kind of variables.
This processes requires a single pass and thus terminates in linear time. Therefore
{\Delta}\vdash_{\mathsf{pre}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}}\;{:}\;{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{\kappa}}}}}
is also decidable in linear time.

We can now use pre-kinding to look at normalisation.
Recall that, by
Fig.

6
, there are essentially four ways to reduce a type
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}
: the usual
\beta
-reduction for an application of a
\lambda
-term (
R-
\beta
); the reduction for the recursion operator (
R-
\mu
); the reductions for sequential composition (
R-Seq1
,
R-Seq2
and
R-Assoc
); and the reductions for duals.
Let us separate reductions arising from
R-
\mu
from the other rules, i.e., let us use
\longrightarrow_{\mu}
for reduction under recursion and
\longrightarrow_{\beta;D}
for the usual
\beta
-reduction as well as reductions for sequential composition and duals.
Let us also extend these reductions to functions on an application, i.e., we have
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T\ V}}\longrightarrow_{\mu}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U\ V}}
,
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T\ V}}\longrightarrow_{\beta;D}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U\ V}}
resp. whenever
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}\longrightarrow_{\mu}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U}}
,
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}\longrightarrow_{\beta;D}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U}}
resp.
In this way, we have
\longrightarrow\ =\ \longrightarrow_{\beta;D}\cup\longrightarrow_{\mu}
. We also extend the notion of weak head normal form and normalisation into the reductions
\longrightarrow_{\beta;D}
and
\longrightarrow_{\mu}
, writing
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}\Downarrow_{\beta;D}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}\Downarrow_{\mu}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U}}
.

Lemma 8
(Normalisation)

If
{\Delta}\vdash_{\mathsf{pre}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}}\;{:}\;{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{\kappa}}}}}
then there exists a unique
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U}}
s.t.
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}\Downarrow_{\beta;D}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U}}
.

Proof

Straightforward extension of the normalisation result for simply-typed lambda calculus
[
57
, Chapter 12]
. The fact that we include reduction under sequential composition and duality does not invalidate the standard proof, since these reductions simplify the type by attempting to bring a type constructor to the front; in particular, the depth of the abstract syntax tree defining the type does not increase along such reductions.

Notice that we cannot extend the above lemma to full
\beta
-normalisation since reduction for the recursion operator may increase the size of the resulting type. The simplest example is
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\mu}}_{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{\textsc{s}}}}}\ (\char 12\relax\alpha{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{\textsc{s}}}\alpha)
, which is pre-kinded as
{}\vdash_{\mathsf{pre}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\mu}}_{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{\textsc{s}}}}}\ (\char 12\relax\alpha{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{\textsc{s}}}\alpha)}}}\;{:}\;{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{\textsc{s}}}}}}
but does not normalise:

{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\mu}}_{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{\textsc{s}}}}}\ (\char 12\relax\alpha{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{\textsc{s}}}\alpha)}}\longrightarrow_{\mu}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{(\char 12\relax\alpha{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{\textsc{s}}}\alpha)\ ({\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\mu}}_{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{\textsc{s}}}}}\ (\char 12\relax\alpha{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{\textsc{s}}}\alpha))}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{}}\longrightarrow_{\beta;D}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\mu}}_{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{\textsc{s}}}}}\ (\char 12\relax\alpha{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{\textsc{s}}}\alpha)}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{}}\longrightarrow_{\mu}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\cdots}}

See
3.2

Proof

Let
\Delta
,
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}
,
{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{\kappa}}
be given. We can first determine whether
{\Delta}\vdash_{\mathsf{pre}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}}\;{:}\;{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{\kappa}}}}}
(
Lemma

7
).
If
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}
is not pre-kinded, then it is also not kinded.
Otherwise we need to determine whether
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}
normalises, or equivalently, whether there is an infinite sequence of reductions
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}={\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T_{0}}}}}\longrightarrow{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T_{1}}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{}}\longrightarrow{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T_{2}}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{}}\longrightarrow{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\cdots}}

By
Lemma

8
, such a sequence would have to contain an infinite amount of
\mu
-reductions, where between two
\mu
-reductions there must be a finite number of the other reductions. In other words, we can construct (any finite prefix of) the sequence

{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}={\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T_{0}}}}}\Downarrow_{\beta;D}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T^{\prime}_{0}}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{}}\longrightarrow_{\mu}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T_{1}}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{}}\Downarrow_{\beta;D}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T^{\prime}_{1}}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{}}\longrightarrow_{\mu}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T_{2}}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{}}\Downarrow_{\beta;D}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T^{\prime}_{2}}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{}}\longrightarrow_{\mu}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\cdots}}

where for each
i
,
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T^{\prime}_{i}}}
is necessarily reached from
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T_{i}}}
after finitely many steps.

In the above sequence, the only possible reductions that can be applied to
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T^{\prime}_{i}}}
are
\mu
-reductions, since
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T^{\prime}_{i}}}
is whnf with respect to the other reductions. If
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T^{\prime}_{i}}}
does not have any
\mu
-reduction, the sequence terminates and we can correctly determine that
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}
normalises. Otherwise if
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T^{\prime}_{i}}}
(only) has a
\mu
-reduction, then it must fit into one of the following cases (this is where we use the restriction to recursion of kind
{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{*}}
):

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T^{\prime}_{i}}}

\displaystyle={\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\mu}}_{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{*}}}}}\,{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U}}}}}}}\longrightarrow_{\mu}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U}}}\,{({\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\mu}}_{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{*}}}}}\,{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U}}}}})}}}}}

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T^{\prime}_{i}}}

\displaystyle={\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{({\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\mu}}_{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{*}}}}}\,{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U}}}}})};{V}}}}}\longrightarrow_{\mu}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{({\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{U}\,{({\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\mu}}_{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{*}}}}}\,{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U}}}}})}}})};{V}}}}}

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T^{\prime}_{i}}}

\displaystyle={\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{Dual}}}}\,{({\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\mu}}_{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{*}}}}}\,{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U}}}}})}}}}}\longrightarrow_{\mu}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{Dual}}}}\,{({\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{U}\,{({\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\mu}}_{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{*}}}}}\,{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U}}}}})}}})}}}}}

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T^{\prime}_{i}}}

\displaystyle={\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{({\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{Dual}}}}\,{({\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\mu}}_{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{*}}}}}\,{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U}}}}})}}})};{V}}}}}\longrightarrow_{\mu}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{({\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{Dual}}}}\,{({\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{U}\,{({\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\mu}}_{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{*}}}}}\,{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U}}}}})}}})}}})};{V}}}}}

Note that, in each of the four cases above, the expression
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\mu}}_{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{*}}}}\ {\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U}}
reappears after the
\mu
-reduction (without change). Therefore, the number of different subexpressions
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\mu}}_{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{*}}}}\ {\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U}}
that might appear in the sequence of reductions is finite, and we can detect an infinite sequence by ‘tagging’ the expression
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\mu}}_{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{*}}}}\ {\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U}}
and stopping once
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\mu}}_{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{*}}}}\ {\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U}}
reappears. Therefore, we can devise an algorithm for deciding whether
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}
normalises: follow the sequence of reductions, terminating: as soon as no reductions are possible, or as soon as we revisit a previously ‘tagged’
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\mu}}_{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}{*}}}}\ {\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U}}
.
